WOMEN'S CHAT.

Norway's Queen.—Queen Maud of Norway grows more and more like Queen Alexandra ss time goes on, and shews too, much of her Majesty's great charm of manner. Queen Maud also possesses considerable tact, and never fails to say the right thing at the right moment. To her little four year old son, Prince Olaf, Ithe Queen is absolutely devoted, but despite all the adoration this small Royal personage receives,he stands no chance of becoming spoilt. Queen Maud has fol- lowed very closely in the footsteps of her illustrious mother, in the management of her nursery, and holds that the children who are taught strict obedience, and who have their time well filled up and mapped out, are the happiest and best. Since her arrival at Christiania, the Queen has greatly altered and beautified the private apartments in the Palace to which she brought much in the way of furniture, china, pictures, books and curios. For all animals—particularly horses- and dogs—her Majesty has a greet love, and King Edward's corona- tion gift of three fine horses must have been a particu- larly welcome one to Norway's Queen. Dress at Ascot.—Very pale grey toilettes were much in evidence at Ascot, and these were more admired than the all-white ones—and certainly cooler-looking. Vivid touches of colour—green generally—were added, the ,• colour being repeated in the hat and sunshade. With ; these particular gowns, very pale grey swede gloves were worn, indeed there is quite a run at the moment I upon pale grey gloves for wear with white, and all coloured frocks. Fewer elbow sleeves were to be seen ; the full floating transparency from elbow to wrist, grasing several gowns. This mode was set by Lady IMary Hamilton—now the Marchioness of Graham—and we owe her a debt of gratitude for we have witnessed enough exhibitions in the way of arms to last a life- time. We are familiar—painfully familiar—with the, ' bony, scraggy varieties ; the leg-of-mutton specimens I and shades varying from yellow to mahogany, but many , of us have yet to make the acquaintance of the pretty, moderately plump, creamy, dimpled aim, beloved by the writer of fiction and so seldom evident in real life. Truly our arms are not our strong point, and on the principle of" better late than never," we should hasten to cover them up from the light of day. New Veils.—A remarkable feature of Ascot, from a woman's point of view, was the very long and very fragile veil, worn by almost all the elegantly dressed women. The fabric is new, a mixture of tulle and chiffon, and very pale blues,silvery greys, and pale pinks I 1 are apparently the chosen colours. In arrangement, the new veil differs entirely from the" fall "of a year ago. It is first draped loosely over the face and hat, then ! caught up behind, the one Jong end being brought down, and lightly twisted round the throat, and finally thrown carelessly over one shoulder. To the woman past her youth, the new veil will proven blessing indeed, I, as the pretty transparency covers in the most delightful , fashion, all hollows and lines about the neck. ; The Forthcoming Sales.—Owing to a somewhat , treacherous late spring, and early summer, very real , bargains will be obtainable at the July sales. Perhaps the greatest reductions may be looked for in the ready- made robe departments, the blouse department, the underwear departments, and he millinery showrooms, I but furs, rain and dust coats, ready made coats and skirts, ca-toat-cas, and sunshades, will be among the articles marked considerably below the original price. In these days a hen one is permitted—even at sale time —to wander at will throughout the large emporiums without hindrance or question,the seeker after"sacrifices" I should find little difficulty in securing them, but she should see to it that a list of all requirements is made I out very carefully before sallying forth and that the sum total to be expended has been, as nearly as possible serluendgui oall save Years.—The tendency for November , to dress May fashion still continues, andj this summer 1 the lingerie hat is being donned by twiny women who

MRS. ASQUITH AT HOME.

Few figures in that region of society which verges upon politics command so general an interest as the wife of the Chancellor of the Exchequer. In the July Pall Mall Magazine,", with the help of a number of beautiful photographs, we get a faithful and intimate personal study of Mr. and Mrs. Asquith in the town house of the family, surrounded by their children, their bobbies, and their books. The entrance to Mr. Asquith's house (No. 20) has an imposing porch with Corinthian columns, and inside the front door one is ushered into a spacious hall, that is not without an air of comfort. The walls in .wooden panels of bold design are painted in white, and the ceiling bears that deep, rich moulding often Mind in the mansions of the Queen Anne arm early Georgian periods. The whiteness of the walls is relieved by the six massive doors of rich dark mahogany, which are possessions to be envied by people who build houses to-day. The caddie-bags with golf-clubs in the corner are an evidence of enthusiasm for the game, but, i curiously enough, they belong not to our host, but to his wife. Mrs. Asquith is often seen on the links at St. Andrews with Lord Ribblesdale. Mr. Asquith is not a skilful golfer, though of late years he has become . an ardent and expert motorist., and the owner of two smart and powerful cars. Passing through the first door on the right; we find ourselves at once in the library, a lofty, comfortable roomp with black oak floor-hoards partially covered by i an Eastern carnet. Above the bookcases, n the white- painted wooden panels, are. hung rare engravings, mainly portraits of the Prime Ministers of England. On the table, amongst a mass of orlicial-looking docu- ments, one notes more than one portrait of Mrs. Asquith, while several of her sketches ornament the room. The tine old Chippendale chairs are piled up with notable Blue-books, and various scarlet dispatch- boxes stamped in gold letters. Just behind the desk, 'and evidently a precious possession, is a large framed photograph of the late Queen, with the following inscribed in her own flowing hand: "Victoria, R. 1., June 22, 1897. 1837-1897." Crossing the wide hall, we enter Mrs. Asquith's morning-room,an exceedingly pretty and dainty apart- ment. One need not be told that Mrs. Asqnith is a fine horsewoman and a keen follower of the hounds ; the rare old hunting prints about the walls reveal the fact to the most casual observer. But you get an impressioa I of a very different kind from a glance at the hundreds of neatly bound volumes in the many bookcases around this room, a selection which betrays not the usual read- ing of the society woman, but that of a keen student of , literature, of humanity, and of the world's progress. Mrs. Asquith's bedroom, facingithe square, has access ' through one of the long windows to a balcony running well out over the porch. That she hunts with the Quorn and with the Cottesmore is plain enough, not only from ' the hunting whips in the rack, but from the many hunt- i sag pictures hung about. Let into the wall over the I heed of the bed is a large Florentine china Madonna i and Child, to which Mrs. Asquith attaches a peculiar ; reverence and admiration, even as to a crucifix and . praying-stool near by. The walls on eitger side are , covered by signed photographs of mementos of Mrs. , Asqnith's more intimate friends and family; and numer- ous photographs of herself, taken with her young son or , daughter in her arms. The telephone by the side of the bed shows that Mrs. Asonith is not only energetic, but that sje is over ready to be called to either of her two yortfg children—Elizabeth, girl of six or seven, and Anothony, the curly-headed bairn of three. Most of us remember that Mr. Asquith by his first wife had five children—four sons, Raymond, Herbert, Arthur, and Cyril. and one girl, Violet ; but this is a happy family where there is no division. A bright, joyous English home, enlivened by his ' children and graced by a woman who practises well and wieely the art of home-keeping, is that of the custodian: of our National Purse, but the cares of the nation leave him all two few hours to enjoy it.

affair in no way interferes the move- ments of the swimmer. Galatea and serges still remain the favourite bathing materials of the average seaside girl, and the Costume at present under discussion is in a thin scarlet serge. It is made as a tunic, fastening over slightly towards the left side, the cellar and skirt being hemmed with a tan-coloured serge, and the trimming being tan and scarlet braid. The short puff sleeves end in a narrow band of the same trimming.

TO-DAT'S RECIPE.

VANILLA CREA sh—Line ft mould with clear lemon jelly, decorate with strips of bottled pine- apple, apricot, peach, and pear, then fill up with the /following mixture: Pitt a gut of milk and the same quantity of cream in a saucepan with 2oz. of sugar and a little vanilla essence. Allow the mixture to come to the lion. then add toz. gelatine, previously dissolved in a little water. t-vo beatPn rolks of tures.' and stir over the fiii eiTY the mixture Is of the consistency or very thick cream. When cooled slightly add a wines glassful of maraschino, and lastly pint of stiffly beaten cream. pour into the lined mould, and set on ice till required.

axe! shook with excitement, she remembered that if Billy's letter had come Richard would be here too. Bat now he was perhaps cashiered. She recalled awful things of such and such as officer dismissed, "the Queen baring no further occation for his services." 'Few words carried so terrible a meaning. Such a phrase was the black cap of military justice. And now she saw tat Richard, knowing his own innocence, must of necessity suspect her. What would he think, or do, or say, or feel ? She must see him or must write ; but first, first of all, she must let Lord Graham know that Captain Blundell was not guilty. She thanked Heavqn they were in town twain, Had they been in the country it would have added to her difficulties. But no, she was not glad. Had they' been in tho,country she could have written, and this must be done at once —now, now 1 In spite of her mother's dragonship, a despotism tempered by sleeping till lunch-time, Madge had much liberty. Her bicycle and the park gave her exercise. She walked by herself, too. She had achieved a certain freedom after much warfare. She found the family at once the - last refuge of tyranny and the first school of revolt. Though the night had inade her pale the sleep following had renewed her. She was very beautiful, vary interesting, even splendid, when her eyes gleamed. And the rush through the frosty air brought her colour back. She drove in'a hansom to the War Office, and prayed she might not meet Sir Dan or Bobby Maitland there: Yet When lie knew, what would- .he say? She asked it a thousand times, and refused to answer lest her courage should fail her when Richard needed it so much. She almost lost her head at, the very beginning when she found herself past the sentry. But she carried herself bravely„ "Is Lord Graham, the Commander-in-Chief, here? " she demanded, panting. "Yes, ma'am, yes, miss," said the young Orderly she .had opened fire on. "Tap me up to his room." - Fortunately for her most of the messengers were away, and the Tommy Atkins she spoke to surrendered at discretion when he saw her eyes. ' He chewed her the room and was for retiring: "Oh, take my card in, please." But now a mighty messenger, a porter, a dragoon, came running. The orderly Jetire_fa routed, ,and almost ran. "What is it, madam ?" "I want to see the Commander-in-Chief," said lfadgi, now gradually recovering. "I'M afraid you can't, ma'am, unless you have an.appointment. Is it on business?" "No," cried ,Madge, almost furiously. " -private." Might it always be so, she prayed. Then she was aware of a softening of the porter, and he drew up at attention. A lithe and springy, bright-eyed man of sixty, with the eye of an hawk, and eiceedingly well dressed, had his hand almost on the handle of the door. "A lady to see you, my lord." A moment's visible irritability gave way on Graham's face to kindly amusement. This then was the Commander-in-Chief. Thank Heaven for the gracious gift of beauty, Madge! She found herself in the room and the door closed, and the bright Lord' of War was offering her a chair., "Oh, please, Lord Graham, did you get my card?" He had not yet had the pleasure, and when he did have it he wondered what this meant. Not married, eh ? Then she could not be the wife of an Indian officer who had come straight to him to get an indulgence passage out. And yet she looked dangerously neat to weeping. He knew they all wept. The Quarter-Master-General often had a deuce of a time with lonely wives who could not afford the money to join their husbands. Bt this was really a beautiful girl. "Well, Miss Gretton, what can I do for you?" He spoke so kindly that Madge instantly put up her veil, made an effort to smile, and burst into. tears. • "I thought so," said the Commander-in-Chief, who now considered himself under artillery fire. "I. beg your, pardon," sobbed Madge, "but 1 can't help it. And I'm so sorry." "Don't distress yourself, I beg," said Graham. But that made her worse. Graham was a man of Considerable experience and no fool. In spite of opposing cliques it is rather rare for a Commander- in-Chief to be quite a fool. Or at any rate to com- mence as one. SO next time he spoke sharply. "No! Stop crying, if .I,Am please," he said, and was gratified to observe that she stopped instantly. Graham stared at her very hard, but dismissed the notion that she was a lunatic. No lunatic could be 89 beautiVil and so sweet. An, stopping at the bottom of the stairs, she said:

."There, mar, I might have put in a word for poor Melville, who did so well in Bunnah. But I daresay I shall see him again." What a happy girl she was then: happy for soma brief moments till furious, cheated old Sir Dan came back to her mind. Would he sit down and take it quietly, or would he go to her mother and say awfu.l. things ? She felt sure one moment that he would do nothing, brt the next she imitgiqd ha might even now be requesting an interview with Mrs. Gretton. was more than .half afraid to go home. Where was Richard—were they- never to meet again ? qrev grief drifted down upon her: her ardour was chilled by a cold wind, lie...would never, never know what she had done for hint. Perhaps he did not care. , Then the gods looked dort and smiled. For in 'the grey grinl itreet, unlighted by any sun, and chill with December mist and fine sifted snow, came warmth and light and ardour and tropic colour. The cold azure -of a ragged break of clouds was heavenly blue ; the chatter and chirp of the sparrows made an angelic chorus of nightingales in rapture ; the song and smmd of inightiLondon an organ pealing thanksgivings. . For 'Richard, Richard Blundell ("my Richard; ,Annette ") came across the way from St. James's- square, evidently going into the War Office. lie did not see Madge ; nay, how should he know her in that veil, but hint she knew in a single heart-beat; she stopped and turned pale, and again was rosy. And as lifer hero crossed the street she cried his nine tenderly to herself, and justified her worship by seeing how impossible it wits for any soul not to adore him. She was his "dear little girl! " Oli, those mountains, - and the great lake and the . flowery meads of Canx and Les Avants, may they he blessed for ever: for there sweet love was born, ii very god upon Olympus. • - But now, was she to grasp the. opportune fruit, ready and golden to her outstretched hand, as she rode in the swift chariot of Time, or should she shriek and let it go ? An outrage against the gods of Amelia Gretton and happiness, or prim propriety _and long waiting and perhaps disaster. Where was he going, and why ? If he went into that office of the grim and regulated. machinery Of .war rine, since he knew not that' his .name was cleared, he might do awful unofficial acts, and reap dreadful crops in the Gazette. She saw a strange picture cf him tweakin.7 Sir Dzn's_nosa rG a Wax %lAA

The Examiner, Saturday, June 30, l9p6.

ST. HELENS NEWS. •

We print to-day a piece of news which will, we feel sure, prove of the utmost interest to many of our readers. Even if St. jielens were far away the news would be good, but it la all the better coming from near by, for the truth of it can easily be tested. "I am alwayg pleased to recommend Doan's backache kidney pills, for they have done me so much good," save Mrs. M. A. Johnson, 72, Eldon-street, St. Helens. "t had rheumatic fever some time ago which affected Imy kidneys and left my back very weak. There were severe pains round the small of my back, which gradu- ally got vrorse,and I was al most doubled up: my back was I so weak that it was a hard matter for me to straighten I myself. I found it difficult toot to get upstairs." "None of the medicine I tried helped me at all, except Doan's backache kidney pills. My son-in-law sent for some of these for me, and it is them alone I have to thank for the relief I have Obtained. " lam indeed grateful for the benefit I hsbe received. from Doan's pills, and I shall not fail to tell others 1 about them. (Signed) NI: Ann Johnson." Doan's Backache Ridney Pills are two shillings and ninepence per box (six boxes for thirteen shillings and ninepence). Of all chemists and stores, or post free, direct from Foster-McClellan Co., 8, Wells-street, Oxford-street, London, W. Be sure you get exactly the same kind of pills that Nlrs. Johnson had.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

UNSATISFACTORY EXAMINATION RESULTS.

Criticlath of the Secondary School

Resented.

THE TEACHING OF SWIMMING.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee was held at the Town Hall on Wednesday. Aldermen Tennett presided and there were also pre- sent :—Miss Williams, Revs. Canon Morley Stevenson, J. Davies and N. , Wilson, Aldermen Evans and Smethurst. Counoillors Fulton Smith, Barton, Sankey, Evans, Ball, Lord, Pemberton, Dr. Joseph and Ellison, Messrs. R. T. Fairclough, A. J. Jolley, C. E. Parker, H. Kingdon, C. S. Bargees, Fl. Plinston, G. Walker, C. H. Unsworth, T. S. Steel, J. Upson and J. Moore Murray (director of education), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), and Dr. Gornall (medical officer). THE LATE ALDERMAN ROBERTS.

The CHAIRMAN said his first duty was a very sad one, end thirwas to express on behalf of every mem- ber of the Committee the great regret they felt at the loss of their colleague. Alderman John Roberts. He was a member of the Education Committee from the Ist April, 1903, until his death, and he vas also a valuable member of the Sitesand Buildings Committee, a foundation manager of St. Peter's school, and a repre- sentative manager of Silver-street school. In all those capacities he discharged his duties in the way which in all his municipal work they had become accustomed tn expect from him. They had found him invariably an intelligent, clear-headed, straightforward, and honour- able man, and by his death the Education Corn nittee, the Town Council, and the town as a whole had suffered a great loss. Ite moved that the Committee place on record an expression of regret at Alderman Roberts's death, and of deep sympathy with the family. Councillor ELLISON seconded, and said that no-one more deeply regretted Alderman Roberts' death. They had always been very dear friends in the Council, and had worked well together. Councillor Dr. JOSEPH nd, Alderman SMETHURST supported the motion, which was carried.

===== 0002601_19060630_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
SCHOOL KEEPERS.

NO MARRIED WOMEN/TO BE EMPLOYED. i The Finance Sub-committee, on the recommendation of the sub-committee appointed to consider the ques- tion of the payment of school-keepers, resolve a) that, in view of probable impending changes in the regulations of the Board of Education governing the accommodation recognized by them in the public elementary schools, it is undesirable at present to make any change in the committee's rules for the work and remuneration of school-keepers ; and (b), that it be made 0, regulation that no married women be appointed as school-keepers. In view of tho foregoing resolution, it was real red (a) that the temporary engagement of Mrs. Ruth Forster (nee Blenkinsop) as school-keeper of the Ladies' School of Industry be continued from the 16th April, 1906, until such time as her successor is appointed, at a salary at the rate of £52 per annum ; and (b) that the Director be instructed to confer With the managers of the Ladies' School of Industry in reference to the apnointment of a school-keeper for the school. In moving the adoption of the minutes Mr. A. J. J0r..1.6v said the regulation forbidding the appointment of married women as school-keeperst simply wit in definite form what had been the practice of the Finance Committee from the very beginning. Councillor SANKEY seconded. Mr. PLINSTON moved that the resolution with refer- ence to the temporary engagement of Mrs. Forster be not approved. It appeared to him that the recommen- dation was an attempt to get out of a somewhat difficult position. Some time ago the committee allowed Mrs. Forster to he anpointed to the position at a wage of 12s. per week. Subsequently, however, the matter was referred back as the arms were not in accordance with the scale of payments to school keepers. To get nut of that position the sob-committee wanted to get rid of this caretaker altogether, simply because she was a married woman. He did not think the com- mittee ought to begin to penalize females for getting married. (Laughter). He did not €,e any reason why this woman should not be able to do the work equally ss well as an unmarried person. If the time should come that she was not able to do the work satisfac- torily they could dismiss her, but he did not think it was right to make a position of that kind retro- spective. Councillor PENIBERTON seconlei. With reference to the application of the new rule he asked if a widow was not a married woman. A MEMBER: No, she has been. (Laughter.) Councillor PEMBERTON : This doss not apply to widows. __

Canon STEVENSON said he very much hoped that the committee would be successful in their new application. Alderman SMETHURST said one was thankful to hear some little praise with regard to the baths. The Chair- man and vice-chairman of the Baths Committee generally got more kicks than halfpence because they could not let all the children go to the baths free and do all the things that anyone happened to want. If the members of the School Management Committee Went to the baths and saw the amount of work the keeper had to deal with they would soon see that the Committee could not afford to let the keeper attend to any teaching. He had quite enough to do already. He (Alderman Smethurst) did not think there would be the slightest difficulty in the Education Committee appointing an instructor of their own, but be wonld have to be under the control of the Baths Committee and the baths keeper. At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee the following report of a specially appointed sub-committee was considered :—The special sub-committee have carefully considered the question of the attendance of nurses at the public elementary schools in the Borongh, and they recommend that negotiations be entered into with the Queen Victoria's , Jubilee Institute for Nurses upon the following conditions viz :—(1) That the Superintendent of the Queen Victoria's Jubilee institute for nurses be appointed school visitor to the Educatiou Committee; (2) that the Superintendent have the direct control of the nurses visiting the schools; (3) that all instructions of the Medical Adviser to the Education Committee and the Director of Education be given through the . Superintendent: (4) that the whole of the nursing staff of the Association be engaged in visiting schools at times to be arranged—each nurse being allocated to a district; and (5) that the Elucation Committee pay to the Superintendent of the Queen Victoria's Jubilee Institute for Nurses a salary of £94 per annum. THE EDUCATT.ON BILL.

Canon MORLEY' STEVENSON, Ar. A. J. JOLLEY. and the DTRECTOR who represented the Committee at the annual meeting of the Association of Education Com- mittees held in London submitted to the General Pur- poses Committee a report which was adopted. They stated :—A brief resolution was submitted by the Executive Committee, through Alderman G. White, M.P., of Norwich. expressing anproval of the two main principleeeof the Education Bill, public control of the schools and the abolition of religions tests for teachers. Considerable divergence of opinion was manifested during the discussion, especially in regard to the second of these so-called principles, and the impossibility of giving really complete protection by law where any strong or deep-seated theological prepossession exists was stated with admirable clearness by Councillor It. Waddington, of Bolton. The propriety of any resolu- tion dealing with a matter which was the subject of political controversy was also challenged, but, in the end, the resolution was carried by a substantial majority, despite general cononrrence in one of the President's comments to the effect that the Bill was chiefly notable for its neglect of merely educational considerations. Another of the President's criticisms had been aimed at Clause 6on account of the practical difficulties in respect of discipline and attendance which it would inevitably produce; Canon Scott, of Salford, accord- ingly moved that the Association ask for its deletion, and this was carried by a large majority. The really representative character of the Association was evidenced by certain retrograde proposals, earnestly urged, but happily suppressed by the firmness and tact of the President and the votes of the majority of the members. A few desired to revert to the old system in respect of pupil teachers, the cheanness of child labour appealing to their economical instincts. A (mite different section desired to place the whole cosy of education on the Imperial Exchequer, forgetful of the evils of a stereo- typed bureaucratic system which the consequential loss of local control and initiative would involve. The association accepted our proposal that the engagements of teachers in Secondary Schools should be determinable only at' the end of a school term and after not less than three months notice. It Was also agreed to approach the Government with 'the view of securing extended periods for the repay- ment of loans in respect of school buildings ; the pay- ments of annual grants by instalments and their increase and the classification of successful candidates at the certificate examinations. The conditions of exemption from attendance at school and the desirability of enforcing attendance at evening classes were considered. The Association committed itself to the opinion that no one under 16 years of age should be exempt from attendance at either day or evening classes. Perhaps when Parliament can find time to think about education this modest instalment of progressive legislation may be granted.

WIDNES WRYIAN'S; THEFTS.

Catherine Fallon, Wellington-street. was charged at Widnes, on Monday, with stealing a shawl, the property of Catherine Fitzgerald. from the lodging-house she kept in Marsh-street. Complainant missed the shawl at 6.30 on Friday night, and next day, after information had been given to the police, the shawl was found at Newport's pawnshop. Defendant was fined £1 and costs. She was also charged with stealing two doors from an unoccupied house in Wellington-street. She pleaded not guilty, and stated that some one had placed the doors in her yard. The bench imposed a fine of £1 and costs.

JUNE 30th 1906.

The Government has at THE length had an opportunity GOVERNMENT of testing its strength as MAJORITY, against all other parties in the House of Commons. On Tuesday, Tory Free Traders and Whole Hoggers, almost the whole force of the Opposition, went into the same division lobby with seventy Irish members, nineteen followers of Mr. KIER Etannxx, several Liberals who are in favour of the secular solution of the education question, and other gentlemen sitting on the 841110 benches who wish to make Clause W. mandatory. The result clan be surveyed with a great deal of equanimity. The Ministerial majority was 103. As Mer- cutio said of his wound. "It is not so deep as a well, nor so wide as a church door—but 'twill serve." What is proved beyond all question is that .the Goverment solution of the religious diffi- culty is the one that commends itself to the great bulk of Liberal members, and therefore, as matters now stand, to the mind of the country. It would have suited us, and we believe it would have suited most of the supporters of the Government, if the Education Bill, which is a document of consider- able length and complexity, could have been confined to its first clause, which says in a manner beautifully direct and simple, "On and after January Ist, 1908, a school shall not be recognised as a public elementary school unless it is a school provided by the local education authority." That would really have been enough. The Bill in that form would have excluded the religious difficulty altogether. It would have taken no note of tests for teachers. There would have been no question of manage- ment by clergymen and their friends. The local authorities would have stepped in, unhampered by 4 any of those doctrinal differences and those clerical ambitions which have for three generations or mole made the education question a battle-ground . for warring sects, and have as one dismal conse- , quence placed us among the backward nations of,, the world in regard to the education of the young: But just becluse this warfare on the subject a education exists, and just because our differences_ lon the subject are so deep as to be almost vital,

it is a practicable and not an ideal settlement that has to be looked for. Years hence—not a • great number, perhaps—the historian of our own times, looking back towards this day, will wonder why we could not perceive that the secular solu- tion was not only the best attainable, but the only one that could be really final, and also the one that would in the long run be insisted upon' by the country. But we are not t yet in the position of the future Stubbs, or Froude, or Freeman. Parliament will have nothing to do with ideal solutions, and for the reasui that it believes ideal solutions are not now required by the country. We are still in the stage of com- promise. We are far from being out of the wilderness of denominational conflict. The ' Government, feeling this, has, been content to make progress along the line of least resistance, being at the sarn,) time convinced that this is, under existinc.l conditions, the nearest possible approach to the line of equal justice. It now knows exactly where it stands in regard to the House of Commons as a whole. It touched bottom on Tuesday in the matter of its majority, and it finds itself, on the most critical portion of the Bill supported by a preponderance ot 103 members.

The first conterence " for THE CULT or THE the cult of the bsautifu i l n BEAUTIFUL, industrial centres" Was held in the 31Ianche5ter Town Ilan on Walnesday,when some fluttering remarks ;Tre made in allusion to the Rev. Morley 'evenson who is the practical founder of the Scheme It is really a kind of nationalisation of 8031etie8 like the " Beautiful Warrington" Society and others with not dissimilar aims. The dis- cussion followed interesting and profitable lines, and it was obvious that the organisation has a promising future before it. , A good deal was MA in regard to the smoke nuisance, which it was generally admitted, had a lot to do with retarding the work vf beautification in all large towns. Dr Markel read a paper on the subject in which he said that the nuisance was due entirely to a wasteful and unscientific consumption of fuel. He declared that, as regarded factory smoke, there was a cure for smoky chimneys. Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons were saving £25,000 per annum by the improvements introduced during the last °few years in the consumption of fuel—" and we have no smoke," he added. Alderman Bennett has long been laughed at in the Warrington Town 'Council for his perennial protests ,agaiost the smoke nuisance, but his dream of a smokeless manufacturing town is nearer realization than the Scoffers ever thought possible. Moreover, he has a powerful co adjutor in Dr. Markel—a man who • claims to have solved the problem. We hope both gentlemen will continue pegging away until they have destroyed (as well as the smoke) the fallacy that smoky chimneys and industrial pros- perity must necessarily go hand in hand.

We are sure that the regret will be mutual at the severance between the Rev. H. Dunnico and the people 'of Warrington- The i young pastor of Golborne- street Baptist Church has been more closely , identified with the life and welfare of the town ; and its inhabitants during his four years ministry . than is at all usual. He has ministered to a congregation much larger than his own Church or even Parr Hall could accommodate, for he has essentially preached the simple gospel of Jesus 'Christ direct to the people. The narrowness of creed or dogma did not bind him to one stereo- typed form of Christian service, and as Alderman kdennett said on Sunday evening, his success was lue " to the way in which he had striven to lead the Dple of this town back to the fundamental prin- ?les of Christianity." Mr. Dunnizo has proved .imself a forceful man as well as a persuasive preacher. He commenced—and we say it in all aindliness—by making enemies, but he has finished with an army of friends at his back. There were in Mr. Dunnioo'a earlier methods, indications of the combative rather than Re still remains' and alvvaYs the Christlike spirit. we make bold to saywll be a fighter, but thati ' vl fig ht with fi ht with more polished weapon he he result of hi t s stay in Warrington. He has learnt, as well as taught, 50M3 valuable lessons, and not:only does he leave Warrington better than be found it, but he takes with him a ripened experience and a broadened sympathy. We, as well as Warrington, wish him well in the new and larger sphere of work which. lies before him at Liverpool. Opportunities make the man, but in Mr. Dunnico's case we are convinced that, with health and strength, he is a man who, will make the opportunities and turn them to the highest and best advantage.

We are not surprised to THE SAVAGE receive a letter of rernons- SUFFRAGETTES. trance against the leaderette we published last week the above heading. It was our intention to 'Peak strongly on the matter, and in doing so we "eyed the anger of those who, like our corres- ident, apparently cannot see that a good cause ling spoiled by bad advocacy. In common with •- great majority of newspapers in the country We are anxious to ditoonrage the kind of methods eoThloyed by the suffragettes. They not only ?flake a nuisance of themselves but they behave Vi 10 a manner which proclaims their own t.nsuitability for the votes they seek. Where is the likelihood of obtaining so valuable a concession t when its most prominent advocates persistently alienate the sympathy not only of the powers . that be, but of the general public in addition ? We are as anxious to see the woman with a stake In the country, and the responsibilities of citizen- on her shoulders, entrusted with the franchise as either our correspondent or the "savage shrieking suffragette." But we deplore and condemn the u.nwolmanliness of those who distnrb public Meetings and make themselves obnoxious to men engrossed with the cares of statecraft. Mr. Osborne says glibly that " we must know that a lady of Miss Billington's reputation does not create disturb; ances out of any desire for pleasure or amusement: It may be ignorance on our part but we do not low anything of the kind. Previously we had :it suspected that Miss Billington and many her co-agitators possessed any reputation at 11 except the somewhat notorious one resultant' .Corn disturbing rmhlic meetings and fighting the Police. It is one of the most regrettable aspects 01 the extravagant " votes for women " crusade pat those who will he in the forefront are the least entitled to lead and voice the views of the t3.X. What authority have Miss Kenney and Miss Illington--two totally unknown persons only a ?:ear ago—to speak in the great name of woman ?'' They are disowned by nearly all the : stablished and respected wom9n'B political and 1°683 fie ordani7ations and we regret that it should • 7 In their power by pure personal foolishness to so. Btiouslv defilade the cause which all thinking "lnen have at heart.

THE REV. H. DUNNICO.

the Government. Theoretically this is as convincing as can be ; whether the Socialists can be convinced, however, is another matter. For all their theorising, the French are individual- istic. The thrift of the people proves it. If this may be said besides to argue tenacity of property, M. Clernenceau is probably as happily inspired in rebuking the Socialists as he was in all those attacks which he levelled at successive administra- tions in the militant days when he won the title of" breaker of ministries." M. Clemenceau is himself an advertisement of individualism. It is, moreover,in the nature of France's allies to applaud when a good word is said in this cause.

The cry against motor-cars DUST grows. On Sunday when DISTURBERS. there is a truce to the battle of life, and Hodge sits in his garden in his best clothes indulging in an afternoon siesta, the modern petrol engine, grunting and snorting, and leaving a nauseous stink behind it, fusses rapidly past his door and, whether he likes it or not, covers him and his little plot of ground more thickly with white powder than is the busiest bee who eaters the calyx of a flower covered with pollen. But, unlike the pollen the pulverised roadway serves no useful purpose. It is all very well for a motorist to reply that the new pastime of covering one's fellow-creatures with dust is enjoyable, and that the quicker the drive the more exhilarating becomes the new sport, but even the Dusty Miller, who was abnormally good-tempered, would have had all his mirth choked out of him had he lived in the present day, when a fate champetre is turned into a perpetual bal poudre. In truth " the dustman has arrived," but he does not bring that dust to which Dean Swift referred when he ' took for the text of a charity sermon, " He who giveth to the poor lendeth to the Lord." "Now, then, brethren," said he, "if you like the security, down with your dust." Of course he meant gold- dust. Neither are the road disturbers of to-day in any way related to the fairies whom certain Irish peasants regard as the cause of dust being raised on a journey,when they raise their can beens and exclaim, "God speed you, gentlemen !" Rather are the road-snorters, in the eyes of wayside victims, regarded, Arabian fashion, as evil jinns.

NOTES AND, NOTIONS.

BEEF TRUST MOTTO.

Motto for the public suggested by the Beef Trust : " To the pure all things are pure."—'• Punch." * * * Will Mr. Asquith resign ? The local branch of the I.L.P. demands the resignation from the cabinet of Mr. Asquith as a secptel to the prosecution of Miss rflillington * * *

A big chance is given to the Walton la)-bye as a result of the disaster on the Ship Canal at Irlam. Cannot some of the incoming vessels be persuaded to unload at Walton and transfer their goods to the rail- way at Warrington ? * * * In the division in Parliament on Tuesday on the amendment to make Clause 4 of the Education Bill mandatory, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., voted w*h about 26 other Liberal members, against the Goverii ment. The Government majority fell to 103.

* * * What must the dust nuisance have been like in the great motor race just concluded in France? The winner (Sisz) completed 750 miles in 12 hours 14 mins. 7 secs., or an average/ pace of 61 miles an hour. This is about the " slickest " annihilation of space the world has so far known. * * *

So far as the Education Committee is concerned the question of teaching the elementary children to swim is settled, and.there is no difficulty anticipated with the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee now that the idea of obtaining the services of the Baths Super- intendent has been abandoned. A special swimming instructor is to be appointed and the adaptable youngsters should soon profit by his advice. There is nothing much easier to learn than swimming and I nothing more difficult to forget. In fact, once a I swimmer always a swimmer may be taken as liter. ally accurate. * *

We should imagine that a nice quqtion of liability is involved in the accident on the Arley weighing machine on Wednesday. A heavy traction engine crashed through the surface-plate of the machine into the pit below,wrecking the machine and bursting an 8 inch water main from Appleton. The engine also sustained some damage and required the best part of the day to be pat on terra firma once more. As the engine was travelling along the highway, and the weighing machine is also in the highway, it looks as if the Corporation would be liable, but one never knows. The only certainty is that a lot of damage was done, for which somebody will hap to pay.

A monster picnic and demonstration of Warrington Liberals will take place at Southport on Saturday, July 14th. Members of the Warrington Liberal Association, the Women's Liberal Association, the Junior Liberal Association,and the Progessive Labour League will be able to get reduced tickets for rail, admission to the Winter Gardens, and tea while friends wishing to accompany the party can obtain tickets for 35. 6d. each on application to Mr. J. Howes jun., at the Liberal Club. There are about 2,000 members of these four Associations so that the dimensions of the picnic are likely to be extraordinary. The Member for the Borough will, of course be present. * * * A very curious case under Mr. Balfour's Act has arisen at Prestatyn, in Wales. Forty-eight years ago Sir Pyers and Lady Mostyn built schools at a cost 'of £5,000, and these schools they now withdraw from all public control. They are paying the whole cost of maintenance, and the arrangement is that a Welsh- speaking master, whose creed will not be inquired into, is to take the upper school, where he will teach the ordinary provided syllabus. In the lower school sisters will teach tlse children according to their own dogmas. The MostT n family are Roman Catholic, and iks the local authority h s decided that new schools must be built Here iS a ca. ewhere one-fifth of the children only belong to the denomination in question. The ratepayers are naturally somewhat averse to finding , additional money for new schools. ,* * * Mr. Lloyd George M.P., has addressed a letter to a correspondent, in which he says :—" My attention ' has been called to a statement reported to have been made at Marylebone Police Court by a Mrs, Pankhurst to the effect that 1 in a speech deliveredtartieocr of ymirn Liverpool incited the unpardonable moles - • lAsquith. These are the actual words attributed to me :—..‘ Why don't you women leave your friends alone, and go for your enemies in the Cabinet, and the greatest enemy of all, Mr. Asquith.' This statement I can characterise as an absolute fabrica- tion. lam at a loss to suppose how any one can imagine that to supplement undignified, silly, and malevolent falsehoods will tend to the advancement of a cause with which, in spite of such discouragement as this, I still remain in sympathy." * * * The Free Church open-air service on Sunday evening was such a success that it obviously filled a long-felt want. The majority of poor people do not go to Church or Chapel, and there re miss the humanising influence of Christian tea jug. If they )I will not go to the Church it is for the hurch to go to them, and the Free Church Council is to be congratu- lated on having led the way to the heart of the" man in the street." The Rev. J. F. Matthews believes that this important individual 'is " a distinctly religious personality?' If so, he has carefully disguised the fact, but we believe be will not be insensible to the intention of these services as explained by the same earnest preacher, who said : "All they tried to do would be based on the assumption that the passer-by was their brother man with similar religious instincts, to whom they could appeal as a reasonable creature, and who ought to be prepared to yield to thewer- suasive efforts brought to bear upon his heart and conscience whereby he might be enabled to live a better life and to realize his true manhood."

The Examiner, Saturday, June 30, 1906.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

LORD SALISBURY TO VISIT GARSTON.

Colonel W. Hall Walker, M.P., at a meeting of the Widnes Division Conservative Association, held in Garston,on Nf onday evening, intimated that the Marquis of Salisbury, who was re-elected president of the association, has promised to pay a visit to Garston in about three weeks' time. ROBED IN A SACK.

Thomas Green, of Bedford Leigb, was at Runcorn on Saturday sentenced to three months as a rogue and a vagabond. On Wednesday Green was arrested for drunkenness, but he deceived the parish doctor and the relieving officer into believing he was a lunatic, and was sent to the workhouse. On Friday he took his discharge, and vas afterwards caught begging, and when taken to the police cell he set fire to his clothing, and had to be robed in a sack to appear in court. ALLEGED STABBINC AFFRAY.

At the Widnes Petty Sessions last Thursday, Peter Andrews of Wellington-street, and John Statetis, of Cromwell-street, were charged with having stabbed John Miller, on the preceding Saturday night. All the parties were Poles, complainant being the only one acquainted with English.—Complainant stated that he went to 16, Wellington-street, where he found the defendants, a man named Darentes, and another man, playing cards. At their suggestion he sent out for six gills of beer, which they drank. Later, Darentes and the unknown man went out. Statetis then asked witness to have a game for sixpence. Upon witness putting sixpelice on the table, Statetis insisted upon a shilling and witness added another sixpence. Statetis then declined to play and, upon witness picking up the coins, claimed them as his own. He struck witness, and Andrews then struck him near the eye with a knife, while Statetis held him by his muffler. He had had to have the wound stitched.— Andrews denied that he was present, asserting that he did not return from Liverpool until eleven o'clock, whereas the occurrence was said to have taken place at 10.15.—Statetis said complainant took a shilling which belonged to him. He prevented him from leaving the house, but did not hit him in the eye, though he struck him once across the head with his hand.—The Bench adjourned the hearing in order that inquiries might be made.

===== 0002601_19060630_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
OPENING OF A NEW ORGAN.

At the Milton Congregational Church, Widnes last Thursday afternoon their new organ, towards which Mr. Andrew Carnegie of Library fame has contributed £2OO, was inaugurated.—A service was conducted by the Revs. J. Pedlar, (pastor), J. Yonge (Warrington), and Mr. R. H. Sewell, 8.A., (Liverpool), in the course of which the organ was unlocked by Mrs. Hodgkinson. " in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost." Subsequently the Doxology and Te Deum were rendered to the accompaniment of the new instrument.—The pastor gave a brief resume of the history of the project consummated that day, remarking that that was an occasion which many of them had not expected to live to see. in addition to the £2OO offered by Mr. Carnegie it had been necessary for them to raise £3OO. He had reckoned that £2OO of that could be secured amongst themselves, and that outside friends might be relied upon to furnish the other £lOO. This had proved to be correct. Many of these outside contributors had pre- ferred to remain anonymous, but he might mention Mr. Sutton Timmis, who was always so loyal and devoted in the interests of all local work which was of a philanthropic kind, had sent N £2O, whilst Mr. NV. H. Lever, who did not often go outside the Wirrall district, had given £23. The Rev. J. Yonge heartily congratulated the congreon- tion on having accomplished the desire which had so long been in its heart. For a very considerable time he said, their aspirations had been somewhat vaguely postponed, but now, at last they were realised, and he congratulated them upon what appeared to bean exceed- ingly fine instrument, so far as he was able to judge of its compass and merits. He hoped and expected that they would find, as the days went on, that it was a very considerable addition to the enjoyment of their worship at Simm's Cross, and he hoped that whatever liability remained would speedily be cleared off. He hoped, and believed that what had taken place in recent years was but an earnest of what was to come. He congratulated them very heartily upon having placed the choir in front of, instead of behind the minister.—The Rev. R. H. Sewell expressed his very warm personal congratula- tions to Mr. Pedlar and to the congregation upon the happy celebration of that day. At a later stage in the service the Rev, gentleman delivered an eloquent sermon.—Tea was subsequently served in the adjoining schools.—ln the evening a recital was given on the new organ by Mr. T. Rimmer (organist of Myrtle Street Chapel, Liverpool, etc.), the items being interspersed with solos.

Increase in Vagrancy.

The fortnightly Meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present Mr. A. R. Norman (presiding), Admiral Jones Parry, Messrs. W. M. Morris, P. Collins, T. Woods, J. Douglas, T. Wright, G. Egerton Warburton, J. Spruce, C. Roylance, J. Hallows, J. Whitley, H. Eyes J. H. Davies. C. E. Linaker, T. Guest, J.B. Chadwick, W. Harrison, J. Pilling, S. Woodward, J. Ratter, G. F. Ashton. (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), T. Cook, W. dePrez, and J. Pearson (relieving officers) and J. Cunnah (Workhouse master). THE CONTROL OF EDUCATION EXPENSE. The NantWich Board of Guardians submitted the following resolution for the approval and adoptiost of the Board :—" That in the opinion of this Board it is desirable that the Education Bill now before Parlia- ment should provide for a popularly elected body as the local Education Authority, and thus secure to the ratepayers more complete control of the spending of the public funds than is possible under the present co-optive system. 'Mr. Morris : I think this is really a matter for the Parliamentary Committee. (Laughter). The Chairman : The Committee still exists, and the Clerk is only waiting for an opportunity to call it together. Mr. C. E. Mr. eddon, M.P., gave evidence before the Joint Committee on Sunday Trading, on behalf of the Shop Assistants' Union on Thursday last week. He stated that the view of the shop assistants was that no employe ought to be compelled to work on Sundays against his will, and that no penalty should be imposed if he declined on conscientious grounds to work on a Sunday. If an employer did penalise such a man he ought himself to be subject to a penalty under any Sunday Closing Mt. The association he represented did not ask for compulsory closing on Sundays subject to the condition he had mentioned. They did, however, attach great importance to one day's rest in seven. THE VIADUCT INSTITUTE.

At a special meeting of the members of the above Institute held in the Reading Room, the chief business was to receive the accounts, and to consider the advisa- bility of making up the accounts in the autumn instead of the summer. Mr. C. IV. Emmett presided, in the absence of the President (Mr. H. D. Earl, J.P.), who had been called to London on business. There were also upon the platform Messrs. T. Ormand, J. P.(Secre- tary). W. G. Smalley, J. Ilarcroft, IV. H. Bailey, W. Davies, G. Bunting, W. H. Teece, J. Anderton (Com- mittee), and T. S. Liversage (assistant secretary).—The account having been adopted Mr.l3ailey moved that the accounts in future be made up to the 31st of October in each year instead of June as heretofore.—Mr. Bar- croft seconded, and it was carried unaniatously.—On the motion of Mr. Ormand, seconded by Mr. Smalley,a vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Emmett for presiding.

ST. HELENS.

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. --- A Complaint from Stockton Heath.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present: Mr. G. Pilling (presiding), Admiral Jones Parry, Messrs. J. gDouglas, T. Woods, T. Wright, G. Egerton- Warburton, J. Spruce, C. Rovlance, J. Hallows, J. Whitley, H. Eyes. J. H. Davies, C. E. Linaker, T. Guest, J. B. Chadwick, W. Harrison, S. Woodward, J. Rutter, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Swinton and W. Diggle (surveyors), J. Farrington (inspector), :and Dr. Adams (medical officer.)

WATER MATTERS AT FRODSHAM. A scheme, for the suppling of the John Fowler Homes at Frodsham with water, was approved. It was pointed out that a profit of about £3O a year would be made by the scheme. _

MR. CHAPPEL AND THE DISTRICT COUNCIL. Mr. Steel sppeared before the Council on behalf of Mr. Chappel to show cause why a house which is in course of erection in Pike-street should not be pulled down, the plans not being in accordance with the bye- laws, a 10ft, instead of a 20ft. passage being shown. Mr. Steel submitted that according to the bye-laws a 10ft. passage was all that was required and also that a 10ft. passage was sufficient and reasonable. Mr. Steel asked the Council to consider the matter broadly and allow the erection of the house to proceed. Unfortunately he feared,there was some bad blood between Mr.Chappel and certain members of the Stockton Eleath Parochial Committee. Messrs. Linaker, J. W. Davies and A.G.Guest,with the chairman and vice-chairman were appointed a special committee to visit the site and report to the Council.

BLOWING OF MEAT. Mr. Reginald Potts, clerk to the Cheshire County Council forwarded a report of the county medical officer of health on the practice of " blowing " meat for human food and added that he hhd forwarded a copy to the Local Government Board with a recommenda- tion that the practice should be made illegal and subject to penalties. THE SMALL PDX HOSPITAL. At their meeting the Isolation Hospitals Committee considered an application from the Runcorn Urban Council for permission to use the District Council's smallpox hospital at Moore. The committee recom- mended that the application be favourably considered provided an agreement could be arrived at with regard to the maintenance and administrative charges at the hospital. The report was approved, and a committee was appointed to draw up an agreement and arrange terms between the two authorities. _ •

A COMPLAINT FROM STOCKTON HEATH. Mr. Hall ows said he had a matter to bring forward in the interests of Stockton Heath. Six weeks ago , a child living in Roman-road was suffering from fever. The people had been expecting Dr. Adams to call and he had not done so. The child was now better but the house bad not been disinfected. If a man who was getting Li a week neglected his duty he would be sacked and it was highly important that men who were receiving higher salaries should do their work. Dr. Adams said he had never received the notificetion of the case. That was the second attack Mr. Hallows had made upon him and he would make enquiries and reply at the next meeting of the Council.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

THE VICXR'S ILLNESS.

For some time the Vicar of Earlestown (Rev. T. R. Matthews) has been in a delicate state of health and he has had to consult a specialist in throat troubles at Manchester. It appears that the vocal chords are much relaxed, Mr. Matthews' usually powerful voice being almost gone. It is not likely that he will be able to take duty for some months.

The SOO day excursionists from Manchester and district who paraded Paris last Saturday —the hottest day of the season 'so far—in their huge char-a-bancs were greeted with the most benevolent good-humour by the Parisians (writes a Paris correspondent of the " Manchester Guardian.") The Lancashire burr filled the air around the Gare _Nord as they hurried up in detachments, hot, shiny, and perspiring. Though the return train was due out at 11.45 only, an hour beforehand corners had been occupied, and men with jackets off and shirt sleeves rolled up were instantly seized with an overpowering desire for sleep, only to be rudely awakened by endless streams of new-comers. Of their impressions at the end of the day most were too tired to do more than express their enthusiasm at the beauties of Paris and how they had enjoyed them- selves. Others had noted that the soldiers were not smartly dressed, that policemen returning home put a cigarette between their lips and meandered along with hands in their pockets. The luncheon c.f seven courses with wine included had been a revelation. The short- ness of the stay was a universal regret, followed by emphatic declarations of the intention to return for a longer period.

LABOUR PARTY'S AGENT.

With a view to taking an active part in the forth- coming revision of the voting lists the Labour Party in the Newton Division have appointed Mr. R.Critchley, of Wargrave-road, Earlestown, as registration agent, to look after their interests. His headquarters will be at the Club rooms, Queen-street. Earlestown. MR. SEDDON, M.P., AND THE EDUCATION BILL.

Mr. Seddon, M.P., has sent a letter in reply to the Earlestown and Newton Free Church Council's resolu- tion disapproving of his vote against the second reading of the Education Bill, and the Council has resolved to send the following to Mr. Seddon :—" That this Coun- cil, having received the reply of Mr. Seddon, beg to say it does not remove their dissatisfaction at his vote against the second reading of the Education Bill. It is of the opinion that on the main principles of the Bill, viz, representation with taxation, and abolition of tests for teachers, he might have voted for the second read- ing and held himself open to amend the Bill in Com- mittee."

CO-OPERATOR HONOURED. Mr. David FOrrester, who lately retired after being President of the Earlestown Co-operative Society for ten consecutive years, has been presented with an illuminated address and a purse of twenty sovereigns in token of the respect and esteem of the members. The Soldiers' Memorial. At a meeting of the Soldiers' Memorial Committee it was reported t at the statue had been sent to the ironfounders to He cast and would be delivered in Warrington in about three months. The Sculptor (11r. Alfred Drury, A.R.A..), has written to say that he regards it as one of his best works and intetids to exhibit the model in the Royal Academy next year. Instructions were given to the Borough Surveyor to prepare the foundation in Queen's gardens. Gallant Rescue at Latchford.

A gallant rescne-f rom drowning was effected at Latchford on Wednesday evening. About eight o'clock three children were walking along the canal bank when one of them, Elsie Barrow (8) fell into the water. A workman, whose name has not transpired, gallantly jumped into the water, but was unable to effect a rescue. Hearing the cries of distress, a man named Thomas Boardman ran to the spot, and plung- ing into the canal succeeded in bringing the child to the bank.

S. GA RL AND, 51 to 55, Bridge-st., Warrington.—Advt. Warrington Man's Bravery Rewarded. . The heroism displayed by John Stockton, a baker's carter in the employ of Messrs. Pierpoint and Co., Bridge-street, who last year rescued three men who had been overcome by poisonous fumes in a Warring- ton sewer, at imminent risk to his own life, has been fittingly awarded. On Monday the Mayoress pinned on his breast the Stanhlpe gold medal awarded by the Royal Humlane Societynr the bravest act performed in the year by any British subject. His bravery was warmly praised by the Mayor and Alderman Tinnion. Stockton had previously received the Royal Humane Society's silver medal, and the medal of the Liverpool Shipwreck and Humane Society.

Cycling Accident.

Henry Armitage, Mayfield-street, Bolton, met with an accident whilst cycling along Knutsford-road on Sunday. When near the end of Wash-lane, the head' of the bicycle broke off, and he was Ditched over the front of the machine and fell on his face, which wag cut in several places. He also had several teeth knocked out. Dr. Langdale was passing at the time, and Armitage was removed to the Infirmary in a cab, where he was attended by the House Surgeon and detained.

===== 0002601_19060630_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
Exhibition .of Water-Colour Drawings.

An exhibition of a particularly interesting character has been arranged by Mr. Thomas Birtles and is at present being hqld at Legh House studio. It consists of a fine c filection of water colour drawings by Mr: Oswald Garside, one of our townsmen who has pined distinction in the world of art. The opportunity of seeing-so many of his later works is being highly appreciated, and during the week many ladies and gentlemen have visited the exhibiti n. Jr. Garside received his early training at the School of Art under Mr. C. B. Aylward and e soon showed himself to be possessed of high artistic talents. His success in the profession has been most gratifying to Warringtonians. Some of his pictures have been hung at the Royal Academy and Paris Salon whilst many have been selected fo, the leading provincial exhibitions. He has spent, a considerable time in Italy and Holland where he has found a wide range of subjects for reproduction with the pencil and the brash.—We shall deal at greater length with the pictures next week.

The Fatality at.the_ Vernon-street Tannery.

An inquest was held by Mr. Brighouse on Monday on the body of John Foster, aged IS, son of Mr. Joseph Forster, managing director of the Old Tanning Com- pany, Mersey-street.—Mr. Taylor represented the [ directors of the Company, and Mr. Pringle, H.M. [ Inspector Of Factories, was also present. It appears that on Thursday night, Forster went into the tanyard to shut off the steam, and to stir up the liquor in a pit with a pole. For the latter purpose he got on to a plank which went across 0.4 pit. The plank gave way, and he was precipitated into the liquor, which was at nearly boiling point. Forster managed to get out, and did not appear much the worse. He then went into the house, which is on the works premises, and had ttibath. He afterwards became ill, and was taken to the Infirmary, whete he died the following morning.—Mr. Pringle suggestel tint in order to obviaterfurther accidents of this kind, the ends of the planks should be made to fit into sockets. He also suggested that as this pit was practically at boiling [ point it should have a chain round. The chain could be easily removed when required. —Mr. F. G. Taylor said the recommendations would be carried out.— Nurse Crowther, of the Infirmary, stated that deceased died at 5.15 on Friday morning from shock.—A verdict of "Accidental death " was returned, and the jury recommended that the suggestions made by the Inspector should be adopted. They also wished to express their deep sympathy with Mr. Forster and family-.-11r. Taylor said he was also instructed to I express the sympathy of the directors of the Tanning ' Company with Mr. Forster and family. Naturally the directors would have a feeling of sympathy for anyone in their employ, but they felt this more so as they had seen this yoang insn grow up, and bec.use this was the second son Mr. Forster had lost in seven months.

SUP CANAL DISASTER.

Irlam Locks Carried Away.

A SECTION EMPTIED AND TRAFFIC STOPPED.

Ihe worst disaster which has yet happened on the Ship Canal occurred about 8 o'clock last (Wednesday) night, when the steamship Cassia, of 703 tons, bound from Manchester entered the wrong lock at Irlam and cdrried away the intermediate gates. The lock was filled to accommodate a vessel approaching from the Manchester side, so that the Cassia set free the water in the lock, and the weight of water above on the other gates caused then to give way. The result was that the water in the higher reach of the canal poured through the lock and lowered the level by 16 feet. The Cassia had a very narrow escape from destruction. M3anwhile the approaching vessel fr-Ina Manchester was suddenly left straniel on the muddy battorn of the canal, all has hid t 3 be shored up to prevent tutning turtle. • , The stretch of canal affected by the disaster is from two to three miles long, and it is expected that all traffic will be at a standstill for a week before the disaster is repaired.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Latchford Wesleyan .Church.

Special services were held in the above place of worship, on Sunday. The preacher was the Rev. A. Cecil Parker. In the afternoon, a musical service was rendered by the Leigh-street Primitive Methodist choir, the soloists being Miss L. Snelling, Miss A. Woodland, Miss A. Bridge and Mr. El. Corrin Mr. A. Spruce was the conductor, Mr. .T. Foster, presiding at the organ. The Rev. A. Cecil Parker gave an address, the subject of which was "Popular Fallacies." At the morning and evening services special music was rendered by an augmented choir. Miss B. Eckersley and Mr. :I. Willett, were the soloists. Mr. E. G. Oldbury conducted, and Mr. W. H. Ford presided at the organ. The services were largely attended, and the collections which were in aid of the New Sunday School Building Fund amounted to £8 10s. 6d. Open Air Campaign. Meeting on the Fair Ground.

The first of a series of open air services conducted by the lVarrington Free Church Council. was held on the Fair Ground, Queen-street, on Sunday evening. Mr. C. E. Parker presided and was supported on the platform by the Revs. S. Pitt, J. F. Matthews and C. L. Stowe. The attention of the public was attracted by the play- ing of hymns by the Legh-street Band and in a short time a laree crowd collected—s crowd composed of a fair sprinkling of women and of characters so divergent as to range from a frock-coated, well-groomed alderman of the borough to a ragged "corner-boy," and from workers in the mills and factories to the ruddy-faced proprietors of shooting galleries who the day previously had been entertaining our invenile population on the fai,. ground. Punctually at eight o'clock the service commenced with the singing of a well-known hymn. The size of the audience momentarily increased, the casual passer-by being impelled oy curiosity to swell the ranks, and as the band strnck up the strains of Cardinal Newman's well-known hymn "Lead kindly light" joined by the hearty singing of the hundreds who had gathered round the lorry which did service as a platform, an unique and inspiring scene was presented. Mr. C. E. PARKER, who had generously provided the hymn books, explained that it was the purpose of the .Free Church Council to hold similar services indifferent parts of the town in the hope of reaching those who were not accustomed to attend any places of worship. The general fault to be found with open-air meetings was that only one tune was to be played, and only one aspect of the gospel presented to the people. They hoped by getting the services of the friends from the different Free Churches that the whole of the gospel in all its fulness and in its different aspects would be pre- sented to the people. The Rev. S. PITT. superintendent of the War- rington Wesleyan Circuit, followed with an apt address, not indulging in any flight of oratory, but in a simple and effective manner pointing out the great truths of the Gospel. Some people, he said, made a great deal of the differences and diversities of opinion amongst Christian bodies but he did not think they were anything like so marked as a great many people felt they were. He felt it his dutv to show forth before the world and everyone the sub- stantial unity amongst the people of God. At present they were one in heart and being and if not absolutery i one n doctrine—and they were nearer to being one in doctrine than many people imagined—the fundamental truths which they preached and believed in were very much alike. Mr. Pitt proceeded to instance as a bless- ing which had followed in the wake of Christianity the care for the lives and well-being of the children. But in this direction, he pointed out, everything was not as it ought to be. There were, he said, more children dying in Warrington than there ought to be. One hundred and forty five out of every thousand children born during the year, died before they reached the age of twelve months. What was the cause ? It WAS not poverty, because in Wiltshire the death rate of infants was only 85 per I,ooo,and there the agricultural labourer lived in the most abject poverty. The fact that Wilt- shire was the cleanest county in regard to the drink bill had a great deal to de with the low death rate amongst infants. Not only must the children be better protected but,they must be taught and influenced, and it was a duty upon all of them to see that the children committed to their care were brought up in the name of and for the service of their Lord Jesus Christ. The Rev. J. F. Marritews delivered an address with his characteristic vigour and enthusiasm. He would wish, he said, that their platform was still wider. He was looking forward to the time when men would be able to stand together with the determination to preach that God was love. He never was a shamed and never would be ashamed of standing up with any man, no matter who or what he was,who dared to stand forth and teach and preach the love of God. They were not coming there to insult the man in tbe street. He believed that the man in the street was a distinctly religious personality and it was their business to touch his religions iestincts until he awakened to the beauty and solemnity which was in the highest and truest religion. All they tried to do would be based upon the assumption that the passer-by was their brother man with similar religious instincts to whom they could appeal as a - reasonable creature and who ought to be prepared to yield to the persuasive efforts brought to bear upon his heart and conscience whereby he might be enabled to live a better life and to realize his true manhood. They were to teach men to recognize their brother man and to know that even though he was a-disgusting devil, I revelling in the filth of the gutter, he was still a man who ought to be redeemed and that they 'were sent as a redemptive factor. How could they love God Whom they had never seen unless they loved their brothers whom they had seen and worked night and day for his physical, spiritual and intellectual redemption? A great number felt very cynical about their brother man and thought birn scarcely worth savinc at all and it did seem some- times as tholigh it would exhaust the grace of God ta, bring some men back to the highest but they must go forth and, in the name of the Father, must lay their hand upon that brother's shoulder and tell him of a better life, sober, truer and purer, which he might live if be willed to do so.
