REV. H. DUNNICO'S DEPARTURE.

FAREWELL SERMON AND PRESENTATION.

Large Gathering in the Parr Hall.

The Parr Hall was crowded in every part on Sun- day evening when the Rev. Herbert Dunnico, who will shortly take up the pastorate of Kensington Chapel, Liverpool, preached his farewell sermon. The occasion was of additional interest by reason of the fact that at the close of the service Mr. Dunnico was the recipient of a public testimonial which took the form of a hand- some roll-top desk, a revolving study chair, and a purse of gold. Alderman Arthur Bennett attended to make the presentation. The great gathering was a striking testimony to the success which Mr. Dunnico has achieved during his four years' ministry in War- rington. Not a seat was unoccupied and many people were unable to gain admission to the building. The service formed part of the celebration of the Sunday school anniversary and the musical portion was there- fore of a special character. The children sang a number of hymns very effectively, Mr. Tom Brough conducting. The organists were Mr. A. Trolt and Mr. S. Bickers. Madame Rathbone sang two solos with great taste and expression, Liddle's "Abide with me " being interpreted in a particularly feeling manner.

The Rev. H. DrsNrco in the coarse of a abort address raid a tribute to the valuable services rendered by Mr. Freeman and Mr. Elliott as superintendent and secre- tary respectively of the Sunday school. When he came to Warrington there were just over 100 scholars; to-day they had only a few short of 300, which considering the disadvantages in the shape of the wretched accommoda- tion, was an excellent tribute to the officers and teachers who had worked so loyally. He would also like to thank Mr. Trow,their able organist,Mr.Brough(and other friends who had assisted at the Parr Hall servt ces.He(Mr. Dunnico) wanted to say as he left Warrington that he could never have done the work that he had done if it had not been for the loyal co-operation and the devotion] of friends who loved him and understood him, who did not misinterpret his actions, and recognized that even if he made a mistake he did it with the best of inten- tions and with a sincere motive. He wanted to leave the church free from debt. There was £l6O owing for the chapel renovation when he came and that had been cleared off. If the collection that night realized £l2 or £l5 they would have more money in hand than was needed to meet all bills. The collection was taken and amounted to £l5 Ss. I

.. -- 1 Later Mr. Minnie° delivered his farewell sermon. IBasing his remarks on 2.Chron. 6-8 "Thou didst well ; it was in thine heart, " the preacher said that no one could read the narrative without being strangely moved. The purpose of David, that of building a temple, was altogether a very pure and generous one, but for several reasons enumerated David was not per- mitted to realise it, but the message came to him "Thou didst well ; it was in thine heart." He should speak to them therefore on unfulfilled ideals, unaccomplished aims and human limitations. An ideal,said the preacher, was an outline picture of possible usefulness. It stood for the power of vision. It was not a wordly ambition but a heavenly inspiration. A man's life was formed coloured, shaped, by his ideas. Character and destiny ' were determined by them. They all bad their ideals or at least had had them, but he could not stay to mention many. Perhaps they had had a great passion to become social reformers and lift the people out of their swinish. drunken habits, and make a protest against the brutes and bailie.; who built up their fortunes on the blood and tears of starving women and children They went out on a great crusade, but they fritind by and by that the very people they tried to help were the ones who had the most to say against them and who cried out : "Crucify him!" Perhaps they had attached themselves to some body of Christians narrow in creed, in outlook, bigoted in theology, in the tond hope that by and by their views might be broadened and their creed made as wide as the love of God. But,alas, they had discovered that men who fought for creeds, generally lacked the spirit and were of all men most unlike Christ. Human life was a long story of unfulfilled ideals. Mr. Minnie° then said that the more noble the ideal the less likeli- hood was there of ever realising it. The people who succeeded in realising their ideals were not the superior people. The bulk of men who succeeded as the world counted success, succeeded because they had parted with their ideals. It might well be asked of what use then were ideals if the noble ones were impossible of realisation? They were useful in many ways, the preacher stated. Pirst, how and why was it that they could never realise noble ideals ? The reasons were legion. Physical and intellectual limitations—they were all hemmed in by innumerable limitations. Then many men were hindered by the spirit of the age, the psychological atmosphere. It was very difficult for a man to realise an ideal if he happened to visit this earth forty years before his time. A man who preached a gospel forty years in advance of the people among whom he laboured or forty years in advance of trust deeds he was called upon to subscribe to, generally received abuse, vulgarity, and insolence or some other form of orthodox or ecclesiastical persecution. Still ideals impossible of realisation were of infinite value because of the influence they had upon the life and soul of the man who cherished them and because the ideals of to-day were the actual realisations of to-morrow. Everything in life they valued, all their sacred liberties, were once ideals. The preacher in con- clusion made a very earnest appeal to all present to •be true to the ideals he had ever placed before them. Neves had he appealed there for them to join the particular Church to which he belonged. He had urged them to be Christians, to follow the ideal Christ, had left them to attach themselves to the Church where they could bestserve God and man and develop the powers of their manhood and womanhood. He appealed to the young men to keep their record clean, to reverence the name of woman, not to listen to or lend themselves to indecent language. He appealed to the parents present to make home a sacred word end to make it the happiest place on God's earth. Perhaps he should never look anon some of their faces again but ere he left them he wished to thank them for all their kindness. Whatever success might wait upon him in the future he wanted them to feel that they had some share in it. He owed them a debt of gratitude for all their trust and lave and although a handful of narrow minded people had caused him much annoyance hundreds and even thousands had given him cause for joy. The church to which he WAR going was one after his own heart. There he could preach a gospel broad as eternity, there he would not be handicapped by narrow trust deeds,but he would be able to offer the hand of fellowship to all who loved Christ. He should pray for them; he hoped they would pray for him. If ever the young men there were in doubt, in difficulty, in red of guidance, let them ever look upon Isim as their friend. Ho urged them to be true, brave and faithful, to work hard, and he bade them good-bye for a little while. THE PRESENTATION

The presentation took place at the close of the service. In addition to the gifts to be formally pre- sented to Mr.,Dunnico by Aldermad Bennett,tkere were on the platform an easy chair and a spirit kittle which had been presented to Mr. and Mrs. Dunnico the previous Thursday evening by -a large number of personal friends. Mr. D. GRUNDY, who presided, said that after listening to Mr. Dunnico's sermon, he could not help thinking that every person present would be more sorry than ever-that they could not keep Mr. Dimr.ico with them in Warrington. It fell to the lot of very few towns indeed to have amongst them so fa'thful and sincere a minister who fulfilled his duties to his God and his people—and during the four years that he (Mr. Grandy) had known him he had done his duty fearlessly and well. He was sure that all who had listened to Mr. Dunnico in that hall and in Golborne-street chapel would agree that sincerity formed one of the chief features of his ministry. I f,as he (Mr.Grnndyi believed,sincerity was the backbone of success then success to Mr. Dunnico in his new sphere might be considered to be assured. He was very pleased to find from a letter which Mr.Dumaico had received from, the secretary of the Kensington Chapel what a broad-minded principal pervaded the church there. The writer stated : I feel keenly that Warrington's loss will be our gain in the truest sense. Your church has the proud distinction of sending you to Liverpool where there is great need of Christian ministers to fearlessly preach a liberal Gospel. You come to us free to preach the Gospel of Christ which is able to save to the uttermost. We are not tied by any, miserable or narrow creeds to please the whims of cranks who by their narrowness make it hard for men to be Christians." Continuing Mr. Grundy said they regretted Mr. Dunnico's departure very sincerely,for be had been a trusted friend and pastor to them. He had always spoken to them for their own good. They might not agree with him in every particular, but he had always spoken to them of peace And if any when they had been so addressed had prepared for battle he (Mr. Grundy) hoped they might get free with their own consciences some day. A certain few of his own church might have disagreed with Mr. Dimino in some respects hut he (the chairman) took it that Mr. Dunnico had been a little in advance in ideals of some members of the Church. They had not throaghly understood him and had not been able to follow bin:), and consequently they had made things uncomfortable. But they were a very few and the testimonial which would be pre- sented to Mr. Dunnico had) been subscribed to by church members and officers, the congregation, and members of the public. (Applause.) Mr. OXFORD (superintendent of Kensington Chapel) said they had been served faithfully for thirty-four years by a man of God—the Rev. E. Walter who had been obliged to relinquish the pastorate owing to advancing years. When Mr. Dunnico preached at Ken- sington for the first time several months ago every member of the diaconate felt sure that God had sent him there and that he was the man who would carry on

(Applause). The Rev. J. S. HUGHES, pastor of Hill Cliffe, expressed his appreciation of the splendid services Mr. Dunnico had done in this town and also his personal regard towards him. He had always felt'sympathy with the social work in which Mr. Dunnico had been engaged. He believed Mr. Dunnico was a man with convictions and he (Mr. Hughes). liked such men because he had a few convictions himself. (Laughter.) Mr. Monaco had stood nobly by his convictions during his ministry in Warringtan and the town was suffering a gnat loss by his departure. He was a type of man that we had not the like in the town. He (Mr. Hughes) had known Warrington nearly thirteen years and he had never known a man come to the town who had been able to move the masses who were outside the churches as Mr. Dunnico bad done. For his (Nlr. He had no doubt that Mr. Minnie() would be successful in Liverpool, as he had just the qualities that made for success. (Applause). Mr. HARRISON, of Kensington Chapel, also spoke. The CHAIRMAN read extracts from a letter from Mr, A. H. Crosfield, M.P. who wrote: " Mr.Dunnico knows fnll well that he has my hearty good wishes and my sympathy in the great and important work he has under- taken in Liverpool and his numerous friends in War- rington will be glad to know that we shall now and again see him amongst us . . . . Ihopeso energetic and courageous and practical a worker in the cause of progress will receive a fitting testimonial on his departure from Warrington." Applause). Alderman BENNETT then rose to make the presentation. He said that probably many of those present would think it a curious thing that be should be occupying that position, but he could onlyassure them that he felt it a great !armour to be asked to do so. lie was a Wesleyan and a Conservative and he was proud of both (Laughter). Mr. Dunnico was a Baptist and a ttadical and he too was proud of both. ((iaughter). It might seem incongruous that two gentlemen holding such different opinions should meet on an occasion of that kind. The fact was that all sincere and earnest men who tried to labour for the good of the community found their agreements were greater than their differ- ences. (Hear, hear.) He had found Mr. Duanico broader than his party or his creed, and in his anxiety to promote the principles of Christianity he bad come into touch with men of other sects and creeds and won their allegiance. He (Alderman Bennett) remembered that when Mr. Dunnico came to Warrington he seemel almost at once to create a certain amount 'ocinterest! excitemetit, and criticism. At the outset he (Alderman Bennett) was a little inclined to be criticaland oynical, but it had always been a rule of his neNer to take things secondhand. He had heard so much about Mr. Dunnico that he decided to go and hear him, and the sermon he listeved to—on the "Wonderful Fountain "—was one that he would never forget as long as he lived. It satisfied him that tae reputation which Mr. Dunnico was making was well-deserved and likely to be per- manent, and he had never had occasion to revise that opinion. He had seen how Mr. Domino had filled the chapel and crowded the Parr Hall, and be had been told by cynics who bad not been to see, that it was all the sensation and excitement of a moment. Well, it had gone on for four years and judging by the crowded attendance that night, it did not seem as if it was going' to be very ephemeral. He could not think that work of that character, which had been followed by so much public support during four years, could be accounted for by any mere sensationalism and clap trap methods of which some pleople accused Mr. Dunnico. He had been able to fill his church while others had been almost empty and to crowd that hall when others had failed to do so. He (Alderman Bennett) did not think it was due to Mr. Dunnico's eloquence, although he bad a high opinion of that, or even to his sincerity, which was evident in every word he spoke and in every line he wrote. It was,he thought. due to the breadth of the doctrine which Mr. Dunnico preached and the stay in which he had striven to lead the people of this town bank to the fundamental principles , of Christianity.' 411 e fact was that people were getting sick of mere creeds and externals, and the reason they were turning away from Christianity was because they preached their doctrines and dogmas instead of the principles which Christ taught and which were as powerful as ever they were. Mr. Dunnico had been brave and bold enough to preach in no faltering voice the great main principles which Christ taught, and had not sought to emphasize doctrinal differences. The consequence was that people had flocked to hear him as they flocked to hear Christ Himself—willingly and gladly, and had been greatly benefltted by all they had heard. It was amazing to him (the speaker) how theologians had built round the gospel such a mess of contradictions and bewildering ideas. The Gospel teas, after all, very eimple—"Loie to God and your neighbour; belief in the fatherhood of God and the brotherhood of men "—and that was what Mr. Dunnico had taught. ,He bad also tried to make it clear that it was not merely heaven that Christians were seeking but that they should build up the King- dom of Christ on earth. There was hunger, want and misery on every hand,and it seemed a crying disgrace to Christianity that these things should be allowed to exist. Yet they did exist and many so-called followirs of Christ were silent. Mr. Dunnico was not like that. Again and again he had spoken for the prin- ciples not only of personal righteousness but of social progress and he had not only preached his doctrines but had tried to carry them out in practice. Alderman Bennett referred to Mr. Dunnico's work on behalf of the Social Service League and also to the Christmas toy distribution to poor children which he organized. In these days, he continued, when such tremendous attacks were being made on Christianity if they fell back on those first great principles which Christ taught infidelity would fail and Christianity would go on to conquer in the strength of the message of Nazareth which, he believed, was destined lo revolu- tionise the world.

ARTIFICIAL GRANITE. A new artificial granite, repoked from Frei- burg, Baden, is made from chips of granite or marble, or both. These are made into a oonsist- ent putty with a suitable proportion of cement, water, colouring matter, and any 'other desired ingredients, and the mixture is shaped in mouilds and allowed to set. The blocks are then im- mersed in water to acquire a certain amount of water. On removal, they are dried and hard- ened in a warm and shady place, after which they are polished.

SUPERHEATED STEAM ON LOCOMOTIVES.

Superheated steam has been adopted on 186 locomotives belonging to the Canadian Pacifio Railway, the superheaters being for the most part designed and built in the company's own shops. The results are stated to have given great satisfaction. The engines have shewn marked economy of fuel, even as compared aith the compound locomotives in use on the same railway. In the case of the latest engines, the boiler pressure is being reduced to 1751 b. in place of the 2001 b. per square inch hitherto the standard practice; and it is expected that there will be a saving in the wear and tear of the ,boiler without any reduction on the hauling capacity of the engine.

HOW FISHES CONVERSE.

The "talk of fishes" is peculiar, as Professor T. W. Bridges has shewn in pointing out the voluntary sounds of these creatures, says Science Siftings. Some part of the bony skeleton is movable, and when it is rubbed against some fixed . part of the skeleton the friction of the rough surfaces sets up a sound. The stickleback gives an illustration. In other fish a murmur- ing sound is produced by the pressure of air upon the valves of the air bladder. In still other fish there are two 'little flexible spines, attached to which is a muscle whose contraction causes the spines. to tap against the air-bladder, and this produces a sound like the roll of a drom. WHY DO STARS SEEM RAYED? •

An attempt to aaiount for the familiar rayed or starlike appearance of the stars when seen by the naked eye sis made by W. Holtz in an article on the "Appearance of Stars." which appeared in & German magazine. He finds that all stars shew precisely the same rays, but that in the case of the brighter stars the rays are plainer and somewhat longer. It is further re- marked that the rays seen by the left and right eyes differ, and that if the head be turned the rays are rotated in a corresponding manner. It is thus concluded that the source of the rays is not in the stars but in the eye itself, the middle of the retina being not perfectly h. rno- geneoue in its sensitiveness.

===== 0002601_19060630_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
DEGENERATE BIRDS.

The ostrich is a bird which has lost the power of flying through long disuse of its wings. It is not so degenerate, however, as the rhea, the emu, the cassowary, or, above all, the apteryx. All these feathered creatures are surface dwel- lers, yet all with indications of having once been fliers. It is interesting to observe how their feathers have become degraded and gone back towards the original hair. In ordinary feathers the barbs are fastened together by little cross- hooks, or barbules, so as to make a firm support' in flying; but these are lost in the ostrich, the plumes being •pendent, loose, and downy. In the emus and cassowaries the feathers have gone still further backwards, the wings }miming no plumes, but only horny shafts, the relics of dis- used quill feathers. The body feathers are very stiff and hair-like.

NEW THEORY OF THE EARTH'S INTERIOR. The earth, whose interior still remains one of the greatest of mysteries, is believed by Mr. Beresford Ingram to embrace three concentric, spheres (spheres within spheres). The solid nu- cleus he supposes to be between 3,000 and 7,000 miles in diameter, and this is surrounded by a liquid substratum, outside of which is the crust, variously estimated at 70 to 2,000 miles in thick- ness. More than two centuries ago, a similar theory, including the slow rotation of the inner solid sphere on a different ,axis from that of the entire globe, was held by pr. Edmund Halley to account for the changes in the earth's magnet- ism. The axis of the nucleus was tliought to have been originally that of the entire globe, and to the change, of course, was attributed the deluge. The earth's internal heat, it is now pointed out, may be accounted for by the fric- tion of the differently rotating bodies.

MYSTERIES OF SOLAR PHENOMENA,

Dr. E Ledger, a well-known astronomer, writing in the Nineteenth Century and After, says that science can teach us very little, com- paratively, regarding those things of which we are most anxious to have information respecting thr. sun. Its distance is so great, in comparison with the power of our instruments, he says, as to reduce our knowledge of its nature and con- stitution almost to a minimum. The sun is so far away that we are still intensely ignorant with regard to it, while the brilliance of its light is such as to be the greatest hindrance to our study of it. obscuring our view of what we long, but for the most part try in vain, to de,inher or explain. BE CAREFUL WHAT YOU SAY.

In speaking of a person's faults, Pray don't forget your own; Remember thcee with homes of g:ass

Should seldom throw a atone. If we have nothing else to do , Than talk of those who sin. 'Tis better to commence at home, And from that point begin.

We have rio right to judge a man Until he's fairly tried; Should we not like his company, We know the world is wide. Some may have faults—and who bas not? The old aA3 well ac young; Perhaps we may, for aught we know, Have fifty to their one.

I'll tell you of a better plan, And find it works full well— To try my own defects to cure, Ere others' faults I tell. And though I hope some time to be No worse than some I know, My own shortcomings bid me let The faults of others go.

Then let us all, when we begin To era'nder friend or foe, Think of the harm one word may do To thoee we little know. Remember—" Curses sometimes, like Our chickens, roost at borne." Don't speak of others' faults until We have none of our own.

Le Decay of Danger Boards.

Complaints are frequently heard from cyclists IA the decayed and dilapidated condition of the warning boards on the summits of dangerous hills. Up to within three years ago these boards, were kept in proper condition by the Cyclists' Touring Club and the National Cyclists' Union—by which bodies they were originally erected. The Motor Car Act of 1903 allocated to the borough and county councils respectively the duty of causing "to be set up sign-posts denoting dangerous corners, cross roads, and precipitous places, where such sign-posts appear to them to be necessary." Hence the appearance of the crop of white posts, bearing red symbols, described the other day by an ignorant cyclist as signs of the Zodiac, which have sprung into existence throughout the kingdom. Un- doubtedly of service to motorists, the red triangles cot7ey no meaning to the ordinary cyclist, and by their very profusion are not associated with the special danger of which lie,clesires to be apprised. Now that brakes are nearly universal, hills that were regarded as dangerous twenty years ago in many cases present no difficulty, but in the case of steep, curving descents it is most desirable that the local authorities should maintain the existing danger boards in serviceable conditi on.

• 'The cost of the upkeep of danger boards to the county and borough councils is only a tiny and infinitesimal sum, and to allow the boards to fall into decay is a gross and inexcusable neglect of duty. In view of the obligation placed on these bodies by the Motor Car Act, any cyclist injured by an accident caused by the absence of these warning signals would probably be able to recover damages from the local authority so neglecting its duties.

A Famous Hill, Climb.

On the 16th inst the Catford Cycling Club held its twentieth oven hill climb up the steep slope of the North Downs at Westerham, Kent, Out of an unusually big entry, 44 riders started and 34 secured certificates by reaching the summit. As usual, the contest was open to riders of any type of machine, irrespective of weight, the only restric- tion being in respect to the ratio of gear and crank length, 10 toNl. A former winner, F. S. Bale, reasserted his prowess as a climber, by winning the scratch race in 4 min. 24f sec., while another well- known scaler, R. Barnfather, was third. A sealed handicap was run in conjunction with the scratch race, which added to the interest of the result. Westerham Hill, which has been the scene of the annual event for some six years, was the locale of the first climb, held in 1887, won on a safety by the now famous motorist, S. F. Edge. On this memorable occasion R. L. Philpot scaled the hill on a 52 in. solid-tyred high bicycle weighing.3s lb. Westerham summit is over 800 ft. above sea level, the length of the hill is 2,942 ft., and the average gradient lin 9.4. The stiffest point on the hill is 7known as Hell Corner. AN \ OLD MAN'S CLAIM.

A gentleman once left a will, by which every young man of a certain village was to receive ten pounds on his twenty-first birthday. Once an old man appeared, and explained that he had just had hie twenty-first birthday, which was on the 29th of February, so that meant he only had a birthday every four years. The old man received the ten pounds, and went home very pleased. From your loving Niece,

A. TURNER.

A HORSE TO SHOE.

I Benny was a little boy. One day he went ! to the smith's shop. What did he want there? , I will tell you. He had a horse which was made of wood. He could carry it under his arm. One day, when he was playing with his horse on the road, it ran over a stone and one of the wheels came off. Benny looked at his horse as it lay on the ground, and he felt very sad. He could not put the wheel on again. At last he said, "I know where to go. I will take my horse to the smith!" He took his horse under his arm and went to the smith's shop. He went right in. "Now, My little man,:' said the smith, kindly, "what can I do for you?" "Can you shoe my horse, please?" said Benny, "one of its feet has come right off." The smith laughed, but did not say "No" to the little boy. He had a kind \ heart. /So he took the horse from the little boy, and soon made it all right again for him. Benny then went on his way home, pulling his , horse behind him.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

_ Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

The Savage Suffragettes. Sir,—Your leaderette under this heading is a remark- able one. vrom the rancorous tone of it, one would imagine that the writer was condemning some unclean thing. The language used is not altogetaer gallant and one hesitates to take him seriously. Even if the ladies do "want mental balance "there is surely no need for him to hope that they may be sent to gaol. Mr. Maddison, who is not unknown in Warrington, said in Parliament the other evening that" they were nothing else than female hooligans.". Now, Sir, you must know that a lady of Miss Billing. ton's reputation does not create such disturbances out of any desire for pleasure or amusement. There can be no great joy in being dragged through the streets by burly policemen. There must be some reasons for their actions, and when you have taken the trouble to inquire what those reasons are you will doubtless treat them with a little more kindness and respect. These women are deeply in earnest, and if they do create disturbances and commit technical offences they are doing so with the object of bringing their cause— votes for women—to the notice of the people. Such actions are self-condemnatory. They cannot expect to get votes by such behaviour. The votes will be got by those ladies who have either held aloof from the move- ment or who have condemned the unladylike tactics of their sisters. They will step in when the dirty work is done. Twas ever thus.—Yours truly, EDMUND OSBORNE. 25, Lovely Lane, Warrington. 2lith June, 1906.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS

Cricket. Owing to the pressure on our spaoe caused by the Walking Day programme, we have been obliged to omit our Cricket Notes this week. •

===== 0002601_19060630_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

The first round of the Warring-ton Works Competition for the Finlayson cup was completed on Saturday and after a rest this week end, play in the second round will be commenced on July 7th. Saturday's matches were not productive of much excitement, in each case the victorious teams winning by a handsome margin. Forshaw's should have met Whitecross 2nd team but they failed to put in an appearance and the wirepullers were awarded a walk over.

Iu the match between Fletchers (11) and Warrington Wire Ropery, Fletchers at once went to the front and a lead of eight gained as the result of the first game was gradually increased until with six men off the green 34 points separated the teams. In the end Fletchers won by 81. The hardest fought game was between W. Makens and T. Bridge. The first two points were divided) and the players kept level until the Bth end when Hickens went to the front and with a good run •carried the score to 13-5. Bridge then took up the funning and at the 24th end peeled the score by 14 all. Hickens gained the odd point.

Orford Tannery were in arrears throughout their game with Rylands (No. 1) who half-way through the match had scored Si points to their opponents 30. The Temaining games were more evenly contested, and at the close of play Rylands had qualified for the second round. The best game of the match was between W. Roughley and J. Makin. After ten ends Roughley tris behind, being 3-9. Two more ends saw him 4.10, when 'llhe came to the front in a surprising manner, and with a good run went to 13-12. He was, however, unable to again score, and lost a hard-fought game by two points.

It is hoed to complete the second round by July 14th, and steps will then be taken to commence the con- solation competition for teams of six a-side. It is intended to run the consolation and the con- cluding rounds of the competition proper together so that the finals of both can be played on the same day.

Many of the local co-operative employs are enthusiastic trundlers and they held their second main for the season on Tuesday afternoon. Play was over the Penketh Tannery green and after some exciting play the winners were placed as follows 1, W. Kay, (owes 6) ; 2, W. Ford (scr) ; 3, A. Molvneux (2); 4, F. Moerfleld (5). The final was particularly interesting as Kay barely succeeded in winning by the odd point.

--- The Warrington Conservatives are fortunate in iaumbering amongst their patrons Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart„ and a silver cup which he presented for competi- tion was played for on Saturday. There were a large number of entries and the handicapping generally was exceiient and productive of close finishes. The cup, and silver medal which accompanies it was won by 1 Holland who, after conceding two points to F. Rutter won ' 15-13. F. Rutter as the second prize winner secured a silver medal. W. Millington (scr) and T. Horrobin (scr) were placed third and fourth 'respectively. The cup presented by Sir Gilbert Greenall to the Junior Conservatives was won by H. Wellaovho defeated J. Wilkinson in the final 15-10.

A muting ef the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr. Billinge pre- :siding. 'These were also present :—Mrs. Clay, Miss Broadbent, Mnssrs. W. Hickman, A. J. Jolley, E. L. Bent, R. Bolton, J. Payton, A. Brooks, G. Mather, J. Cook, W. Flannagan., T. Ritchie, M. W.-Seton, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate School).

- , LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD INSPECTOR ATTENDS. 'I he CLERK introduced Mr. Elias, the successor to Mr. Moorsotfi as assictant inspector to the Local Govern- ment Board, who attended the meeting. The CHAIRMAY welcomed Mr. Elias and expressed the hope that his relations with the Board would be of the saracreordial character as those of his predecessor. A GUARDIAN'S BEREAVEMENT. The CHAIRMAN said that since their last meeting one ef their most rsieemed colleagues—Mr. S. Davies— bad been called to -pass through a very severe bereave- ment in the death of his dear wife. Mr. Davies was a very cold and respected Guardian and was probably the oldest Guardian for the Borough of Warrington. He (Mr. Biillinge) was stre he would be expressing the feeling of the Board when he moved a vote of condol- ence witlt Mr. Davies. Mr. BOLOOKY seconded and the Board signified their approval by standing. THE COTTAGE HOMES. At a meeting of the Cottage Homes Committee, applications for the position of foster mother of No. 3 home were considered, and it was agreed to ask the following to attend the next meeting of the Committee : -IMiss Alice A. Bennett, Mrs. E. 11Iaddock, Miss Mary Walsh. and Mrs. A. Brooks ; their reasonable travelling expenses to be paid out of the superintendent's petty cash. The report of the Inspector on the work of the school and its general condition was further considered, and it was agreed that an additional teacher was neces- sary, and that accordingly an advertisement he issued setting forth that one be appointed to live outside, the salary to be for a certificated woman teacher at the rate of £O5 a year, and for an mcertificated teacher 243. The minutes were approved on the motion of Mr. 1 MATHER, seconded by If rs. CL AY. atOF. COMMITEE. At a meeting of this committee M:ss Hedgecock was appointed probationer nurse in the plaoeof Miss Stewart on the usual terms. The granting of leave of absence to I the inmates on Walking Day was left to the discretion I of the Master.

AN UNC:A:AL REQUEST. A letter was read from Michael McDermott, now chargeable to the Guardians at the Lingfield Colony, asked the Guardians to emigrate himself and family to Canada. He said the Salvation Army offered reduced passages to men like himself and men had gone from that colony and were doing well. The CLERK. said the Board had not so far sent people to Canada and the matter would w int a great deal of consideration. Mr. BROOKS moved that the matter be referred to the House Committee and said he was not sure whether a little more money expended in that direction would not be well spent. Mr. Max-FtEn seconded and it was agreed to. At a House Committee meeting held after the Board meeting the master was instructed to ascertain the cost of complying with Mc Dermott's request. VIE EXTENSION QUESTIoN. The CLERK said it would be remembered that the Board instracted Messrs. William and Segar Owen to prepare plans for tte erection of additional buildings. The Local Government Board consented to the loan but the Guardians ultimately deferred further procedure for twelve months. More than twelve months had now expired and Messrs. Owen wrote asking what their position would be. They had a bill of £lO9 2s. 6d. and the effect of their letter was that assuming at some future time the new block of buildings would be carried out, credit would be given in respect of the present account. While it seemed to be perfectly correct, it was, he thought, fair that the House Com- mittee should consider the whole matter. At the close of the Board meeting the House Com- mittee considered the matter and unanimously recom- mended the Board to pay the acconnt,

London, who had been annOunced to speak. Pro- ceeding he said the society had been in existence for a year and „they had perfect right to ask what they had done. Naturally they had attended to organization' and had established branches in every ward in the town. He regarded that as a most important step as it was evident that the people who lived in any particular part of the town were the people who were best able to say what wanted doing in that part of the town, and he thought the different branches would render very great assistance to the work of the society, Com- mittees had also been appointed to attend to the different objects which they had in mind. The plans of the committee were now complete and he looked forward to some very useful work being done in the near future. They had done a little tree planting and he thought they would agree that where trees had been planted a great improvement had been made. Canon Stevenson explained that a plan was being prepared of the parts of the town in which trees could be planted. They wanted to plan out the town so that the work could be done by degrees, and he hoped it would lead to their having a tree-planting day upon which as many people as possible would plant a tree. Canon Stevenson also illustrated the work of the society in the encour- aging the use of window boxes. He was sorry to say that one great drawback to window boxes was that some people had stolen the plants from them and from the front gardens of peoples' houses. Of course no member of the society would think for a moment, of doing such is thing and he asked the members of the society to let it be known that if there was a person who was anxious to have a plant and could not afford to buy it if he would come to the society they would do all they could to help him, and he appeal to him not to dis- courage others who were working to make the town beantiful,by robbing their gardens and window boxes. The flower show which they proposed to hold in September was not for rich people who had highly paid gardeners but for workmen aed children to whom prizes would be given to encourage them in growing flowers. He would like to refer to the backyards. Travelling between Manchester ond Liverpool he saw many back yards. Some were very pretty and others were, well-- ! He hoped people would consider the condition of their backyards and realize what excellent places they could be mild°. He wanted them to dream with him one or two dreams. In Oldham they were thinking seriously of making a garden city suburb. U they could do that at Oldham why could they not do the same in War- r ington, and instead of the houses being put down just as the builder liked, make a suburb where the working man could have a nice little home in healthy surround- ings and with gardens ? It was well worth considering He had learnt another idea from Philiadelphia. There builders were approached to allow vacant plots of land to be used as gardens until such time as they were built upon. Why should they not approach builders to allow vacant land to to be used as gardens on condition - that the plants were taken away when they wanted to build? That again was a plan worthy of consideration. Canon Stevenson urged the desirability of people keeping the streets clean by refraining from throwing paper, S:c upon them and pointed out the work of the society in e this direction by the issuing of circulars to every house - cl holder. He also referred to the smoke question and . expressed the hope that the valuable information col- lected by Dr. Markel would resulttin good work being commenced. He had an idea that if they could only get the stokers of the town '!ogether,and have a tea and talk and imbue them with the idea that as a point of honour they should make as little smoke as possible a very great improvement would be effected. Canon Stevenson also emphasised the work of the society in inducing children to take an interest in the movement and expressed their indebtedness to Mr. Longshaw for ,his work as secretary of the juvenile branch. In con- clusion he said that alt' ough the first year had been one of organizing work it had not been a year without good work. They had been moving and were moving and he thought that before very long they would see definite results. (Applause). They must all do their best to kindle in the town a spirit of love for the beautiful and then they could be assured that in a few years they would see in the wards of the town a very different aspect from that presented to-day. (Applause). OTHER ADDRESSES. Mr. BECK said he was very glad that the society had started the herculean task of making Warrington beautiful and he thought that in the first year of their existence they had done very well. The things to which they had to turn their attention were—first, smoke second, dirt in the streets ; third, dirt in the homes; and fourth dirty people in the streets. The conditiori of some of the Warrington streets was inferior to thr of many towns he had visited. That was of cour, within the area of the Town Council and to draw the attention of therouncil to that matter would not, be thought, be very difficult. He had been down certain. streets in Warrington and seen what was common to all towns—dirty homes—and that they wanted to eradicate. In one district which he had visited certificates We given for the cleanliness of homes and a great impOl:n ment had thereby been effected. The condition of the it .t in the streets was also not peculiar to Warrington was peculiarly British. He knew of no country on the continent and in America in which dirty men travelled about as they did in Englexrd. It seemed to be a peculiarly British custom for a man to leave his work in his dirt and to remain dirty for hours and honrs. Fir sincerely trusted that the anticipatory term Beata& I Warrington would become concrete and would e the region of fact as the result of the work of society. (Applause.) Mr. E. J. HA LL spite with special reference to tree planting and the use of window boxes. The society ia its tree planting did not wish to encroach upon the work of ithe3Town Council whose duty it was to plant trees in the main strets and public gardens. What they wanted to do was to get into the more squalid parts of the town and to bring a little beauty where it was most needed and where it was most absent. In fact their work was to try and make desolate places rejoice and to make the wilderness blossom as the rose. (Applause.)

CAPTAIN CROSFTELD AND THE SN.i.OKE NUISANCE. _Captain CROSFIELD dealt briefly with the smoke nuisance. What was the good, he asked, of living until they were 90 or 100 if they woke up in the morning in the midst of smoke and grime feeling like a piece of chewed string? (Applause). It was impossible for anyone to retain his vitality if he lived in a smoky atmosphere. The question was what could they do to prevent this ? They were apt to put the blame upon the manufacturer. No doubt he was a great sinner, but they were equally great sinners, and by the use of tho hest coal and the careful !attention to the Putting of coal on fires they could do a great deal to bring about an improvement. He advocated evolution and not revolution, and hoped the Smoke Abatement Committee would go 'thoroughly into that question in the coming year, as there was no doubt it was about the most important which they had to tackle. (Applause). Mr. J. MooRE Mt:llTR' spoke briefly upon the forma- tion and the work of the the juvenile branch of the society. The branch was formed, he explained, because the' knew that very little would result from the efforts of the parents unless the children were taught to respect and takh care of the trees and flowers planted. Eighteen months ago there was nothing in their schools in the way of ornament and the visitor would now bestir prised to find the window-ledges with boxes containing beautiful flowers. He appealed to the prrents to assist them. Unfortunately they got the assistance of the parents where it was least needed, and where it was 'Zesired it was little given. (Applause). Mr. H. N. HOUGHTos referred to the disfiguring of the walls of the town by the striking of matches, and urged that somethirr, should be done to stop the practice. The Rev. J:HOWARD suggested that by public sub- scription they should form a departmont where poor people could go, and being properly accredited, have plants given to them. This suggestion was endorsed by Mr. DARLINGTON, who said he thought the Society could only justify its existence by giving help to poor people in whom they raised an inspiration. (Hear, hear.) ALDERMAN BENNETT AND ARTISAN

DWELLTNGS. Alderman BENNETT, in moving a vote of thanks to the Chairman and officers for their past services, said he thought they had every reason for encouragement, and that during the first year it had been in harness the Society had amhly justified itself. (ffear, hear). He desired to refer to workmen's dwellings. The absolute ugliness, nay, the indescribable hideousness of the average house of the artisan class baffled descrip- tion. It was ndt necessary that they should be so uniformly ugly, as be was sure there was a suqicient capacity amongst the architects of the town if they used their brains to build a house at a reasonable rent, which was not so atrociously depressing and out of harmony with other buildings which they saw in their towns. (Applause). Mr. W. H. J. JEKtivs, in seconding, heartily com- mended the operations of the juvenile branch. The vote was heartily accorded, and a brief reply by the Chairman concluded the meeting.

Death of Mrs. H. A. Thompson. , We record with n.gret the death, which took place- on Friday niallt at Baurnemouth• where she wls spending a holiday, of Mrs. Thompson, wife of Ur. K. A. Thompson, the Warrington 'agent for the Midland - Railway. An inquest on the body of the deceased lady was held on Saturday, when a verdict of "Death from heart failure" was returned. Mrs. 'Thompson was apparently quite well when she•left 'Warrington on Thursday at midnight, and it is believed that the hot weather may have had something to do with her suddden collapse. The body was brought to Warring- ton on Monday, and interred in the Cemetery ott Tuesday. Much sympathy is felt for Mr. Thompson • and his family in their sad loss. WORKINGMEN'S MISSION :—established 1871; number of scholars on books, 689, boys 315. girls 337 ; average attendance, 486 ; number of teachers, 43 ; superintendents. Mr. T. Dale and Mr. W. Worrall ; president, Mr. N. Elough • vice-president, Mr. G. Dale, The children will assernbfe at school at 9 a.m., and journey to Mr. Cooke 's Farm at Burtonwood. Four lurries will be provided to convey the infants. Two large banners and fifty small bnes will be carried in the procession. Tents are being erected on the field and there will also be refreshment stalls. The children will

enjoy themselves by playing all kinds of games, includ- ing football, cricket and skinning rope. At night the scholars will return to school to be supplied with their usual gifts of nuts and oranges.

BUCKLEY-STREET MISSION.— Number of scholars on books, 550 boys and girls ; average attend- ance, 370 ; number of teathers. 12 ; superintendent, Mr. Jennings; assistant superintendent, Mr. T. Cook ; hon. secretary, Mr. Ryder ; assistant secretary, Mr.H.Wooley. Headed by the Legh-street Band and the school banner the children will join the procession in the Park and march round the town. Dinner will be served in the school after which the children will proceed to a field near Orford Barracks. Games and races for prizes will be indulged in and tea will also be provided. BRICK-STREET MISSION :—Established. 1893. Number of scholars on books, 22 boys, 211 girls ; average attendance, 350; number of teachers, 24. This mission has a free ministry ; superintendents, Mr. Edward Roberts and Mr. James Shaw ; secretaries, Messrs. W. Yates and S. Gallop. The children will leave school at nine o'clock headed by the Mission Band and large banner with the illustration " The Guardian Angel," and will walk round the town. After- wards they will adjourn to a field at Padgate. Dinner will be served at 12.20 and tea at 4.30. In the afternoon

the annual cricket match will ta.ie—pl;Csei;;;;‘i.f,; married men and single men. The scholars will start back from the field about 7.30.

Padgate Walking Day.

Padgate's annual childrens festival was held on Saturday, and was highly successful. The morning was fine and although the afternoon was dull at times, ,the conditions were very agreeable. The united procession set off from the centre of Padgate at 2 p.m., headed by the band of the Cottage Homes under Mr. Yorke. Then followed the children of the Church Sunday School, led by their beautiful banner, which was surrounded by prettily attired maidens holding variously coloured ribbons flying from the top of the banner. The younger children were conveyed in three lorries. Next came the children of the Cottage Homes,accompanied by Mr. and Mrs. Wright and the officers. Their neat appear- ance and orderly marching were much praised. The lorry conveying the juniors was very tastefully decorated in red and white and the children on it also were similarry coloured favours. Next came the teach. era and scholars of the Wesleyan Sunday School headed by their banner, the cords of which were held by six little girls who looked very pretty in their summer dresses. Another lorry containing little children brought up the rear of one of the prettiest processions seen in I Padgate in recent years. The route throughout was lined with spectators. Mr. G. Mather, acted as marshal, The children returned to their respective schools for tea on the conclusion of the march, and afterwards pro- ceeded to their respective fields where sports, games, &a., were indulged in with great zest for several hours. Their pleasure, however, was brought to a premature conclusion as rain began to fall heavily about eight I o'clock and this was succeeded by one of the worst thunderstorms which has visited Padgate in recent years Although many of the children and adults were soaked to tlittskin it is fortunate that nothing of a more serious nature occurred.

===== 0002601_19060630_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====

1/4

The Thunderstorm.—Damage at Warrington. Warrington was visited by a severe thunderstorm on Saturday night but fortunately little serious damage was occasioned by the storm. Rain fell in torrents and cellars in the low lying streets were flooded, and in some districts the streets also were several inches deep in Water. The flashes of lightning were frequent and vivid and the electrically charged atmosphere had a considerable effect upon ;the trains the service being only continued with difficulty. Only one breakdown is to be recorded the feeder cable, on the,section over the crossing being fused with the result that passengers had to be taken in one car to the crossing alight and board another car on the other side of the railway. Mr. Mathias, electrical engineer, was soon on the spot, and the breakdown was remedied as quickly as possible. Three men had a narrow escape from being seriously if not fatally injured at the Patteb Arms Hotdl. They left the vaults at about ten o'clock, when the coping stone of the large chimney facing the station weighing about 2lcwt. fell to the ground with a loud crash, one of the broken pieces of stone rebounding and striking one of the men on his knee. Fortunately he was not much, hurt, but the escape of the whole of the men was miraculous.

BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON.

ANNUAL MEETING OF THE SOCIETY.

The Work of the Past Year.

THEFTS FROM WINDOW BOXES.

The annual meeting of the Beautiful Warrington Society was held at the Parr Hall on Wednesday even- the Rev. Canon Morley Stevenson presiding over a fair attendance.

THE REPORT.

Mr. John Fairhurst presented the annual report as follows :—This society was inaugurated at a large and representative meeting held at the Town Hall on Tuesday, 27th June l 1905. It was the outcome of an article in the " Manchester Guardian," on Beautiful Oldham. Thc article was brought to the notice of one of the secretaries of the Warrington Society, by Canon Stevenson,with a view to the establishment of a similar' ; society in Warrington. The suggestion met with approval ; a small committee was appointed to draft ; the constitution, and to arrange for the meeting previ- ously mentioned, which proved very successful. It was I thought most fitting that Mrs. Higgs, the founder of I the Beautiful Oldham Society, should be invited to address the meeting. A number of members were enrolled, and from that date the society has done much Ito improve the town, although it has been so short a 'time in existence. Like many other new undertakings it met with severe criticism and ridicule, and many prophesied that it would be a failure. Some 16,000 circulars of two kinds have been distributed Ito the householders and workmen of Warrington,l setting out the objects of the society, and asking for their co-operation, and about 12,000 others, inviting all to take part in keeping the streets tidy, were also sent out. The Society are hopeful that these communi- cations will eventually bear fruit. The membership has steadily increased,the latest numbers are 335 members and 130 helpers. It has been decided by the Executive that inabilit to subscribe shall not be a barrier to anyone wishing to join the Society, and; an intimation to this effect was made in one of the circulars. Three pieces of land, in Hazel-street, in a street near Fairfield Hall, and in Knutsford-road, have been planted with trees and shrubs. Some of the trees have died,and will be replaced later, but on the whole the planting wasisatisfactory. The Hazel-street land was handed over to us to plant, and the owner also bore the cost of planting, and the Knntsford-road land has been lent until such time as it may be required for building upon. The Corporation have planted trees in Knutsford-road, also along the river side, and on the Island. Trees have ; been planted all round the space near Arpley Statioaby the London and North-western Railway Company. and ; a piece of land near Bank Quay Bridge has also been I planted with trees and shrubs by the same company. For these improvements we have to thank Mr. I. T. Williams, the District Manager. Some owners of property have planted trees in the new streets, and others have made proposals to the Council that they were willing to provide trees in other new streets. nn improvement has also been made in several of the Church yards by the planting of trees and shrubs; St. Paul's can claim to be the pioneer in this matter. A large number of trees have been planted round the Recreation Ground of Messrs. Gros- field and Sons, at Sankey, and when the time arrives for thinning these out, the society Will receive some 300 to 400, if places can be found for them. The Corporation have agreed to try the experiment of placing wire baskets in the streets for waste paper, and also on the tram-cars, for waste tickets. For the purpose of divid- ing the work, and placing the members in closer touch with each other, the town has been divided into Wards, each ward having its own chairman and secretary. By this means,we are hoping in due time to see substantial results. It is very gratifying to the Executive to find such a ready response by the inhabitants, the great object being that the work should be carried out by individual effort rather than by a few. During the year we have had two public lectures in the Co operative Hall, the first on the Smoke Abatement," by Mr. J. W. Kershaw, of Waterloo, and the second on "How to make green oases in dingy places," by Mrs. Leo Grindon, of Manchester. Both these lectures were well attended, and the hall was lent free of charge on each occasion by the Co-operative Society. We are also indebted to the Press for the notices they hav,e given from time to time, and the space they have so generously set apart for any communications bearing on ; the subject. The matter of smoke abatement is receiv- ing attention. A large amount of information has been gathered from other towns, and later this information will be placed before the Society, and some practical suggestions made. There are many signs that some of the seed sown has taken root. If no further evidence of the work done could be given, the fact that we have enrolled 2,500 children as juvenile members of the Society who pledge themselves not to damage trees or shrubs, and to prevent others from doing so. as far as they, are able, would justify our existence. This, as can be Aeeu is a very important branch, and the thanks of the Society are due to the teachers who have identified themselves with this section of the work, and especi- ally to Mr. F. H. Longshaw, the Secretary of the School Children's Branch. Each child will be presented with a plant, to be grown at home, and at a time to be , arranged it will be brought to the School for competi- tion, the best from each school will be further judged at the annual Show, to be held on the Ist September. , ; Attention may also be called to the window-boxes 'which are being fixed in every part of the town ; this; is an outward and visible sign of the fruits of our work. Arrangements have been made for the supply 1 of window-bexes, plants, and all necessary material, at a price which comes within the reach of most people. DepOts will eventually be opened in each Ward, if practicable, where it will be possible to receive plants and materials for distribution to the members and eventually become an exchange. In con- I elusion, the thanks of the society are due to the many kind friends who have assisted to carry the work so far, giving so; generously 'of their time and substance in inaugurating this movement for the beautifying of our town and generally to stimulate a love\for the beauti- ful. 'ln this connection I should like to name the Rev. Canon Stevenson, and Dr. Markel. FINANCIAL REPORT. Mr. JOSIAH LONGLAND, hon. treasurer, presented the annual balance sheet. The receipts from subscriptions I amounted to 19s. Gd. in addition they had received £lO from the owner of the land in Marshhouse-lane towards the expense of the land being taken over and planted by the society, which together with smaller items brought their total inc'4me to s£6o 2s. Id. The priccipal items of expenditure had been printing, advertising, postages, &c. The planting of trees had cost £0 is. 2d., and manure carting, &c., .£0 Os. 10d. There was left a balance in hand of .£ll 13s. sd. The subscriptions varied from £2 2s. (1) to Is. -(250). With reference to the balance in hand it would be more than absorbed by one or two accounts which had not yet been pre- sented. If the society was to carry on its work effec- tively they would require every much larger number of ; subscribers or else an increase in the amount subscribed 'by the members. In many cases subscribers were pre- pared and were expecting to be called upon to pay an increased amount. So far the work of the society had been almost entirely of an educational charm* and as the money spent in printing S:c.,wouldnot occur' again more money would in the future be available for the actual planting of trees. They proposed that year to hold a show to denote the progress of the society. That would cost about e5O and they would have to be ; prepared to meet the exbense so he hoped that the members of the society would respond as generally as was in their rower during the coming year. (Applause). Alderman BENNETT said he thought the finances of the society were fairly satisfactory. Of course if they extended their operations they would have to extend their resources, but no doubt the hint thrown out would be fruitful.

Councillor FULTON Smini said that many of the subscribers of Is. paid that amount with the idea of becoming members of the society and were prepared to pay an increased subscription. The Rev. HARVEY COOK asked if it was necessary that their income should increase. He thought it would be better to ask for say £75 a year than to appear to be dragging more money from unwilling pockets. Canon STEVENSON : The income required will largely depend upon the work undertaken. If the society undbrtakes half the work I would like it to undertake we would want an income three or four times as big as at present. (Applause.) The reports were unanimously approved. , ELECTION OF OFFICERS. '

Canon STIIVENSON expressed his indebtedness to the secretary and treasurer for their services. He did not know how they could possibly have carried on the work of the society had it not been for the exertions of those two gentlamen. On the motion of Mr. DARLINGTON, seconded by Mr. L. GREENING the hon. secretary and the hon. treasurer were re-elected and heartily thanked for their past services.

--INIK-I.ONGLaN'D and Mr. FA.IRHURST both returned thanks.

Greening, E.J.Hall, W. Silcock, J. Moore Murray, J. Upson and Ryan were appointed to act as the committee. A hearty vote of thanks to Canon Stevenson for pre- siding concluding the meeting. NATIONAL FREE CHURCH COUNCIL AND SUNDAY OBSERVANCE. Rey. J. Scott Lidgett, M.A., President, and Rev. Thomas Lair, Secretary, of the National Council of the I Evangelical Free Churches have issued the following appeal to the Secretaries of the 806 local Councils of England and Wales : As you are probably aware a Committee has been formed, including representatives of all the Churches in this country and of many other organizations, to safeguard Sunday observance in this country. It is proposed that a united effort shculd be made in November next, and that preparatory sermons on the subject should be preached in the Churches and Chapels on Sunday July lat. • On behalf of the Executive Committee of the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches w,e most earnestly invite the sympathy and active co-opera- tion of all Free Churchmen in this matter. The churches cannot be indifferent to the many efforts which are being made to rob the nation of the priceless posses- sion of Sunday rest and the sacred influence of divine worship and quiet family communion. Will you immediately communigate with all the ministers of Free Churches in your Council area, in order to induce them to deal with this subject in a preparatory way on Sunday, July Ist ?" COMMERCIAL FAlLllRES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette,': the total number of Commercial Failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, June 15th, was 139, viz. :—New Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, 74; Deeds of Arrangement registered, 65. The I respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were : Bankruptcies, 72 ; Deeds of Arrangement 76—total 148; being a decrease of 9. The total num- ber of Commercit.l Failures recorded during the 24 weeks of the present year is 3,992; the total number recorded in the corresponding 24 weeks of last year was 4,275, showing a decrease of 283.
